1	// Built-in slash-command registration for the app layer.
2	// Discover only reads markdown files from disk; built-in descriptors such as
3	// /context and /review have no file, so callers must register them explicitly.
4	// NewSetWithBuiltins keeps that step in one place: app builds the set through
5	// this helper and routes the matching slash input to the built-in hook. The
6	// `argent commands` listing path in internal/cli stays unchanged and keeps
7	// listing only on-disk commands, so no user-visible output changes.
8	// NewSetWithBuiltins discovers commands from sources and registers the built-in
9	// descriptors (currently /context and /review). Explicit markdown commands win
10	// by name because Add never overwrites an existing entry.
11		// A .md saved with Windows CRLF line endings must parse exactly like LF.
12	// Package commands discovers markdown slash commands and expands their bodies.
13	// A command is a `.md` file in `.argent/commands` (project) or
14	// `.argentgo/commands` (user). Optional frontmatter carries `description`,
15	// `argument-hint`, `allowed-tools`, `model` and `aliases`; the body is the
16	// prompt template.
17	// The substitution rules match the Rust implementation:
18	//   - `$ARGUMENTS` / `$@` — everything after the command name;
19	//   - `$1` … `$99` — one whitespace-separated argument;
20	//   - `${1}` — the same, when adjacent text would swallow the digits.
21	// Quoted arguments ("two words" or 'two words') count as one position. With
22	// ExpandOptions.RemainingAtHighest, the highest-numbered position used by the
23	// body absorbs every remaining argument, matching the markdown-command
24	// convention. The safe default keeps the original one-argument-per-position
25	// A “ !`command` “ span runs a shell command, but only when the caller opts in
26	// with ExpandOptions.AllowShell; by default the span is left untouched.
27	// ProjectSubdir is where a repository keeps its slash commands.
28	// HomeSubdir is where the user keeps commands shared by every project.
29	// Command is one command discovered on disk.
30		// Name is the slash-command name, including any namespace prefix.
31		// Aliases are alternative names accepted by CompleteCommands. They come
32		// from the frontmatter `aliases` field (comma-separated); an empty slice
33		// means the command has no aliases.
34		// Description is the one-line summary shown by `/help`.
35		// ArgumentHint is the usage hint (frontmatter `argument-hint`).
36		// AllowedTools narrows the tool allow-list for this prompt.
37		// Model optionally pins the model for this prompt.
38		// Agent optionally pins the agent/persona for this prompt (frontmatter
39		// `agent`). It is exposed for the caller to apply; this package never
40		// builds an agent.
41		// Body is the template, without frontmatter.
42		// Path is the file the command came from.
43		// Plugin is the plugin that provided the command, empty for project and
44		// user commands. It is also the name's namespace prefix.
45		// Warnings reports invisible characters found in the declarative content.
46		// It is metadata only: parsing never changes and never blocks on it.
47	// Source is one directory to scan and the namespace its commands get.
48	// Prefix is empty for project and user directories. A plugin source sets it to
49	// the plugin name, so its `review` command is reachable as `plugin:review` and
50	// cannot collide with a user command.
51	// DefaultSources returns the command directories for one session, highest
52	// precedence first: project, then user.
53	// Set is the resolved command list for one session.
54	// Discover loads every command under sources.
55	// Sources are searched in order and the first command with a given name wins,
56	// so a project command shadows a user command of the same name.
57					// A malformed command file cannot participate in discovery; the
58					// error is reported by ParseStrict for callers that want it.
59	// Parse reads one command file. A file without frontmatter still works: its
60	// stem is the name and its first non-empty line is the description. Malformed
61	// frontmatter is skipped leniently; use ParseStrict to report file and line.
62	// ParseStrict reads one command file and reports invalid frontmatter with the
63	// file name and the 1-based line number of the offending line.
64	// List returns every command, sorted by name.
65	// Len returns the number of commands.
66	// Get finds one command by name.
67	// Expand renders a command body with the arguments the user typed, using the
68	// safe defaults (no shell interpolation).
69	// ExpandWith renders a command body, honouring the options.
70	// Expand is a convenience wrapper around Set.Get + Command.Expand.
71	// ExpandOptions controls the optional parts of body expansion.
72		// AllowShell enables `` !`command` `` interpolation. It is off by default
73		// because the markdown file may come from a plugin the user has not read.
74		// Dir is the working directory for interpolated commands.
75		// RemainingAtHighest makes the highest $N referenced by the body absorb
76		// every remaining argument. Off by default so existing templates that use
77		// $1 and $2 as individual positions keep their meaning.
78		// Run overrides how interpolated commands are executed (tests, sandboxes).
79		// It receives the command text and returns its output.
80	// substituteArgs expands $ARGUMENTS, $@ and $N/${N}. Positions are parsed with
81	// quote grouping so "two words" remains a single argument.
82	// dollarToken parses one $-prefixed token at i.
83	// splitArguments splits a command line on unquoted whitespace. Quotes only
84	// group arguments; they are never executed as shell fragments.
85	// fields is the minimal frontmatter reader this package needs. It is a copy of
86	// the skills parser's scalar half: importing that package would couple two
87	// otherwise independent directories.
88	// parseFieldsStrict validates frontmatter syntax before reading values, so a
89	// malformed line can be reported with the line number it came from.
90	// splitFrontmatter is the commands-local copy of the fence splitter.
91		// Normalize CRLF first, mirroring skills.SplitFrontmatter: on Windows a
92		// file saved with CRLF has an opening fence of "---\r\n", so without this
93		// the prefix check below fails and the whole frontmatter leaks into the
94		// body. LF-only text is unaffected.
95		// Full-width ASCII typed through an IME must match half-width candidates.
96	// Command-argument completion.
97	// CompleteArgument is a pure matcher for a single slash-command argument: it
98	// never reads files, spawns processes or mutates its arguments. Candidate
99	// values are derived from the command's frontmatter `argument-hint`. A hint
100	// token whose text (after stripping one optional <...> or [...] wrapper)
101	// contains a '|' is treated as an enumerated choice, e.g. `dev|stage|prod` or
102	// `[dev|stage|prod]`; each alternative is a candidate. Plain placeholders such
103	// as `<env>` or `[file]` describe an open-ended value and yield no candidates.
104	// CompleteArgument returns the candidate values from cmd's argument-hint that
105	// begin with argPrefix, in hint order. Matching is case-insensitive and
106	// rune-safe (full-width ASCII folds to half-width, so CJK IME input works) but
107	// the returned strings are the untouched candidates, ready for insertion.
108	//   - An empty argPrefix matches every candidate, so one function serves both
109	//     the empty-query listing and incremental completion.
110	//   - Whitespace-delimited hint tokens are inspected independently; a token
111	//     with a '|' contributes its alternatives, others are ignored.
112	//   - One optional <...> or [...] wrapper is stripped before splitting, so
113	//     both `[a|b]` and `a|b` are recognised.
114	//   - Empty alternatives and duplicates are dropped; a group with fewer than
115	//     two distinct values is not an enumeration and is ignored.
116	//   - A command with no hint, or a hint made only of open placeholders, has no
117	//     candidates and yields nil; a non-empty set of candidates with no match
118	//     for argPrefix also yields nil.
119	// argumentKey normalizes a candidate or a user-typed prefix for comparison.
120	// Unlike completionKey it keeps '/' and embedded spaces: an argument may be a
121	// path or a multi-word value.
122	// argumentCandidates extracts the enumerated values from an argument-hint.
123		// Values holding whitespace cannot round-trip through an argument hint,
124		// so with fewer than two simple candidates they reach level 3 (identifier).
125		// Case-folded sort key, original string as tie-breaker, exact duplicates
126		// removed; the same order is produced on every call.
127		// "/cmd ze" is command-shaped but no command is named "cmd", so level 1
128		// fails and the trailing token completes through level 2.
129	// Completion coordinator: aider-style three-level fallback over a flat pool of
130	// available strings.
131	// CompleteCommands and CompleteArgument are deliberately single-purpose pure
132	// matchers (command names on one side, enumerated argument values on the
133	// other). Complete ties them together with the fallback order aider uses in
134	// its input widget, so a frontend needs one call instead of reimplementing the
135	// normalization and the "which list applies right now" decision in every
136	// language binding. Nothing here reads files, spawns processes or mutates the
137	// caller's slices.
138	// Complete returns the completion candidates for input drawn from available.
139	// The three levels are tried in order; the first level that produces a match
140	// wins, so an ambiguous prefix yields the whole list of matches (the
141	// "ambiguity list" a dropdown needs to render). Levels:
142	//  1. Command names — when input starts with '/', the available strings are
143	//     treated as slash-command names and matched with CompleteCommands. That
144	//     reuses its leading-slash handling, case/width folding, CJK-safe rune
145	//     matching and alias rules; as in CompleteCommands, a partly typed
146	//     argument ("/rev --all") does not suppress the name matches.
147	//  2. Command-specific candidates — the trailing token (or the token after a
148	//     command, as in "/deploy st") is matched against available through
149	//     CompleteArgument's enumerated-value rules. A trailing space after a
150	//     complete slash command ("/deploy ") yields an empty prefix, i.e. every
151	//  3. Identifiers — anything CompleteArgument cannot represent (values holding
152	//     whitespace or '|', or a single candidate) is matched as a plain, CJK-safe
153	//     prefix. This is the aider fallback for file names and chat identifiers.
154	// Matching is case-insensitive and width-folding (full-width ASCII and the
155	// ideographic space behave like their half-width forms), so Chinese IME input
156	// works at every level. Results are deduplicated and stably sorted by their
157	// folded, lower-cased value with the original string as tie-breaker; a nil or
158	// empty input lists everything in available. The returned slice is fresh and
159	// never aliases available.
160			// Level 1: CompleteCommands ignores anything after the first token, so
161			// a partly typed argument ("/rev --all") still completes the name.
162	// completeCommandNames adapts the flat candidate pool to CompleteCommands by
163	// wrapping each string as a command name, then maps the matches back to names.
164	// completeArgumentNames adapts the flat pool to CompleteArgument by encoding
165	// it as one enumerated argument-hint group. A value containing whitespace or
166	// '|' cannot round-trip through a hint token, so such values are left to the
167	// identifier level. A group needs at least two distinct values to count as an
168	// enumeration; a single candidate is likewise handled by the identifier level.
169	// completeIdentifiers is level 3: a plain prefix match using the same folded,
170	// case-insensitive key as argument completion. It keeps '/' and embedded
171	// spaces, so paths and multi-word identifiers complete sensibly.
172	// lastToken returns the portion of s after the last whitespace rune.
173	// sortedUnique returns a fresh slice sorted by folded key (original string as
174	// tie-breaker) with exact duplicates removed. It never touches its input.
175		// Deliberately unsorted input, with a command matching by both name and
176		// alias, and two commands sharing a name from different paths.
177		// The "review" command matched by alias must appear exactly once.
178	// Command-name completion.
179	// CompleteCommands is a pure prefix matcher for slash commands: it never reads
180	// files, spawns processes or mutates its arguments. It exists so a UI can offer
181	// a live suggestion list without re-implementing the package's naming rules
182	// (namespaces, frontmatter aliases, CJK input) in every frontend.
183	// CompleteCommands returns every command whose Name or Aliases begin with
184	// prefix. Matching rules:
185	//   - A leading '/' and surrounding whitespace are ignored, so a half-typed
186	//     slash input such as "/rev" works directly.
187	//   - Only the first whitespace-delimited token of prefix is considered; a
188	//     partially typed argument after the name does not suppress matches.
189	//   - Comparison is case-insensitive and rune-safe (multi-byte CJK names such
190	//     fold to their half-width forms, covering input from Chinese IMEs.
191	//   - Aliases declared in frontmatter match as well. For a plugin command an
192	//     alias also matches with the namespace, e.g. alias "re" of "acme:review"
193	//     is reachable as "acme:re".
194	//   - An empty prefix matches everything, which makes one function usable for
195	//     both the empty-query listing and incremental completion.
196	// The result is a fresh slice sorted by Name (then Path for stable ties) and
197	// never contains duplicates, even when a command matches via several names.
198	// Complete is the Set convenience wrapper around CompleteCommands.
199	// completionKey normalizes a user-typed prefix or a command name/alias into a
200	// single comparable form. Width folding is done rune by rune before case
201	// folding so a full-width Latin letter behaves exactly like its ASCII form.
202	// foldWidth folds full-width ASCII and the ideographic space into their
203	// half-width forms. It is shared with argument completion so both kinds of
204	// input accept text typed through a Chinese IME.
205	// Every documented section appears, including per-doc truncation reasons.
206	// JSON is valid, carries the same normalized values and stable keys.
207	// Empty input still renders every section without panicking.
208	// Pathological input cannot inflate the report: lists are capped and names
209	// are clipped, in both the text and JSON views.
210	// Sorting and clipping make the report deterministic even when the caller's
211	// slices arrive in a different order.
212	// The built-in descriptor and hook follow the /review registration pattern.
213	// Built-in /context: a read-only explainability view of the model context.
214	// Commands in this package are data (Command descriptors plus hook structs),
215	// not a Go interface, so the core logic lives in the pure builder
216	// BuildContextReport. A built-in hook (ContextHook) and a markdown command can
217	// both call it without this package importing internal/app or internal/agent.
218	// All inputs are injected as plain slices/strings, keeping the function
219	// deterministic, stdlib-only and easy to test.
220	// Bounds keep a pathological input from producing an unbounded report (the
221	// report itself can end up in the context it describes).
222	// MemoryDoc is one instruction file currently injected into the system prompt.
223	// Bytes is the length of the injected text; Reason explains a truncation.
224	// IgnoreStatus is the outcome of hierarchical .argentignore discovery.
225	// ContextInput is everything needed to render a /context report. It contains
226	// only plain data so callers own the app/agent state and this package stays
227	// layering-safe.
228	// contextReport is the normalized, bounded form shared by both renderers.
229	// Field order is fixed, which also makes the JSON output deterministic.
230	// normalizeContext sorts, bounds and clips the input. Slicing copies keep the
231	// caller's slices untouched, so repeated calls with the same input are stable.
232	// BuildContextReport renders a compact, human-readable "what is in context"
233	// view. Every section is present even for empty input. The output is bounded
234	// and deterministic: the same input always renders the same text.
235	// BuildContextReportJSON is the machine-readable twin of BuildContextReport.
236	// It uses the same normalized input, so both views always agree.
237	// clipContextText bounds s to max bytes without splitting a UTF-8 rune.
238	// ContextCommand is the built-in slash-command descriptor. Registration is
239	// the caller's job (internal/app): set.Add(ContextCommand()) after Discover
240	// and route `/context` to ContextHook.Run, mirroring /review. This package
241	// never builds an app or reads the filesystem for the report.

243	// caller supplies the ContextInput it owns; Run stays pure and never errors.
244	// Run implements the hook signature used by the built-in command hooks.
245	// Command-discovery diagnostics.
246	// Discover and Set.Add deliberately drop candidates whose name is already
247	// taken, and ParseStrict rejects malformed frontmatter. That keeps the resolved
248	// Set small but hides why a command disappeared. The functions here are pure,
249	// read-only inspectors for callers that want to surface those decisions (doctor
250	// output, UI warnings, tests): they never read files, mutate their input or
251	// change discovery.
252	// DiagnosticKind classifies one command-discovery problem.
253	// Diagnostic is one problem found while inspecting command candidates.
254	// Name is set for name-related diagnostics. Winner is the path of the
255	// higher-priority candidate discovery keeps for a duplicate name or source.
256	// Line is the 1-based frontmatter line for syntax errors, zero otherwise.
257	// DiagnoseCommands reports every problem in a priority-ordered candidate list.
258	// The first candidate with a given name or path is treated as the winner,
259	// matching Discover and Set.Add. Nothing is filtered or modified; a nil or
260	// empty list yields no diagnostics.
261	// Callers should pass the un-deduplicated candidates (one per parsed command
262	// file, sources in priority order) so shadowed commands remain visible.
263		// Per-candidate validation, in input order.
264			// A body that reads a positional placeholder should say what the
265			// position means. Named placeholders ($ARGUMENTS/$@) are self-describing
266			// and need no hint.
267		// Shadowed names. Grouping by trimmed name also catches a name that only
268		// differs by surrounding whitespace, which Discover keeps but Get resolves
269		// to the first entry.
270		// The same file contributed twice (e.g. two sources pointing at one dir).
271	// DiagnoseCommandText diagnoses one raw markdown command file. It is the
272	// frontmatter-aware entry point: a syntax error becomes a Diagnostic carrying
273	// the file and its 1-based line, while parseable text is inspected by
274	// DiagnoseCommands. No file is read.
275			// Defensive: the checks above accepted the text, so this cannot happen;
276			// keep the result a diagnostic rather than a half-parsed command.
277	// pathKey normalizes a path for duplicate-source comparison. On Windows the
278	// filesystem is case-insensitive, so case alone must not hide a duplicate.
279	// highestPosition mirrors the token scan in substituteArgs so diagnostics and
280	// expansion agree about which $-placeholders are real. It expands nothing: a
281	// diagnostic is only a read-only observation.
282	// Clipping must never split a multi-byte rune: a finding's Detail ends up in
283	// the human-readable report (and in prompts), so leaving a partial UTF-8
284	// sequence there produces invalid text. Strings are normalized with Fields
285	// first, so embedded whitespace is collapsed before the byte budget applies.
286	// The same guarantee must hold for a finding produced by the real diff path,
287	// where a long Unicode TODO line flows through redactFinding + clipFinding.
288	// Built-in /review: a read-only inspection of a dirty git tree. The only
289	// commands it may issue are `git status --porcelain` and
290	// `git diff --unified=3`, both through an injected runner so callers can
291	// enforce the read-only boundary and tests need no real repository.
292	// reviewLargeDiffLines flags a file whose diff adds an unusually large number
293	// of lines. git status alone cannot report file sizes, so diff volume is the
294	// read-only proxy used here.
295	// CommandRunner runs a single read-only process and returns its stdout.
296	// Implementations should reject any command other than the two git reads
297	// Review asks for.
298	// RunnerFunc adapts a function to CommandRunner.
299	// Run implements CommandRunner.
300	// Finding is one compact review observation. Detail is always redacted.
301	// ReviewReport is the result of a read-only diff review.
302	// String renders the compact, human-readable findings report.
303	// Review inspects the working tree with two read-only git commands. It never
304	// writes files, stages changes or shells out to anything else.
305	// ReviewCommand is the built-in slash-command descriptor. Registration lives
306	// in internal/app; call Set.Add(ReviewCommand()) after Discover and route
307	// `/review` to ReviewHook.Run.
308	// ReviewHook is the registration seam for the /review slash command. A caller
309	// supplies a read-only CommandRunner (typically backed by internal/proc) and
310	// invokes Run with the user's arguments.
311	// Run executes the review and returns a prompt-ready findings report.
312	// Add registers built-in commands into a discovered set. Explicit commands
313	// from markdown files win; Add never overwrites an existing name.
314	// quotedSecretPattern covers the `apiKey := "value"` shape that
315	// security.Redact's simple key/value scan can miss. The local pass runs first
316	// so the quoted value is gone before the general redactor rewrites the line.
317				// Context lines occupy a line in the new file too.
318		// Back up to a rune boundary before appending the ellipsis, otherwise a
319		// cut in the middle of a multi-byte rune leaves invalid UTF-8 behind.
320	// Rich-text summaries of commands for help screens and completion lists.
321	// FormatCommandSummary is a pure capability: it formats the declarative fields
322	// of a Command, reads no files, spawns no processes and never mutates cmd.
323	// FormatCommandSummary renders cmd as a bounded-width, one- or two-line
324	// summary suitable for a help screen or a completion dropdown.
325	//   - The "head" is cmd.Name followed by cmd.Description, separated by two
326	//     spaces; empty fields are skipped (a name-only or description-only head is
327	//   - When both the head and cmd.ArgumentHint are non-empty, the summary has
328	//     two lines: the head, then the hint indented by two spaces. Otherwise it
329	//     has a single line holding whichever part is present; if every field is
330	//     empty the result is "".
331	// Each line is truncated to at most width terminal cells (display columns).
332	// Truncation is rune-safe: it never splits a UTF-8 rune and never cuts an East
333	// Asian wide/full-width rune in half — CJK ideographs, kana, Hangul, fullwidth
334	// forms and emoji count as two cells and are included only when both cells fit.
335	// Combining marks and other zero-width runes count as zero cells. Embedded
336	// newlines and repeated whitespace collapse to single spaces so a field always
337	// stays on one line. A width <= 0 leaves no room and yields "".
338	// summaryField normalizes one declarative field to single-spaced, single-line
339	// text so the summary never grows an extra line from the input.
340	// stringDisplayWidth returns the number of terminal cells s occupies.
341	// truncateToWidth returns the longest prefix of s whose display width is at
342	// most width, cutting only at rune boundaries. Wide runes are never split.
343	// runeDisplayWidth is the number of terminal cells a rune occupies: 0 for
344	// control, combining and zero-width runes, 2 for East Asian wide/full-width
345	// runes (and emoji), 1 otherwise. The ranges cover the CJK blocks relevant to
346	// Chinese, Japanese and Korean command descriptions without a dependency.
347	// wideRuneRanges is a compact subset of Unicode East Asian Wide/Fullwidth
348	// blocks (and wide emoji), sorted ascending for inRanges' early exit.
349	// zeroWidthRuneRanges covers combining marks and format characters that do not
350	// advance the cursor on their own.
351	// Language-prefixed verify commands.
352	// Verification commands can arrive as a flat list of strings, following aider's
353	// --lint-cmd convention. Each entry is either a bare command or a command
354	// preceded by a language name and a colon:
355	//	"go: go vet ./..."
356	//	"python: flake8 --select=E"
357	// ParseVerifyCommands turns that list into a language -> argv map. It is pure:
358	// it reads nothing, spawns nothing and never mutates its input. The split is
359	// deliberately conservative so a colon that belongs to the command (http://,
360	// C:\tools) is never mistaken for a language separator.
361	// Argv slices come from strings.Fields, matching how config.VerifyCommands is
362	// parsed, so the values can be handed straight to the app verification allowlist.
363	// Quoting and shell metacharacters are therefore not interpreted here.
364	// VerifyAllLanguages is the map key for a command with no language prefix.
365	// Aider applies such a command to every language; callers should use this key
366	// as the default when no language-specific entry matches.
367	// IssueKind classifies one verify-command parsing problem.
368	// Issue is one problem found while parsing verify-command arguments. Index is
369	// the 0-based position of Arg in the input slice; Message is a stable,
370	// human-readable explanation suitable for doctor output or UI warnings.
371	// ParseVerifyCommands parses a flat list of verify-command arguments into a
372	// language -> argv map. Each argument is interpreted as:
373	//   - "lang: command" — a command for one language (or file extension). The
374	//     language is trimmed, lowercased, stripped of a leading dot and
375	//     canonicalized through the alias table below, so "go", "golang" and
376	//     ".go" all share the key "go".
377	//   - "command" — no language prefix; stored under VerifyAllLanguages.
378	// The separator is the first colon that is followed by whitespace (or ends the
379	// argument) and whose left side is a single language-like token. A colon
380	// followed by "/", "\" or any other non-space character stays part of the
381	// command, so "http://example" and "C:\tools\vet.exe" remain intact.
382	// Invalid entries are skipped (they produce no map entry) and described in the
383	// returned issues. When two entries normalize to the same language the later
384	// one wins and an IssueDuplicateLanguage is reported. The returned map is
385	// always non-nil; the argument slice is never modified.
386	// splitVerifyArg separates an optional language prefix from the command text.
387	// emptyLanguage is true only for a leading ":" (a malformed prefix form), which
388	// must not be silently reinterpreted as a bare command.
389	// validVerifyLangPrefix accepts a single language-like token: letters, digits
390	// and the punctuation used by real language names (".go", "c++", "c#",
391	// "objective-c", "node.js"). Spaces and quotes disqualify a prefix, which then
392	// makes the whole argument a bare command instead.
393	// verifyLangAliases mirrors the alias groups used by app.verifyLangForms so a
394	// key parsed from the CLI normalizes the same way as a config map key. This is
395	// a local copy because importing app would invert the package dependency.
396			// The previous binary is kept next to the new one; a running exe cannot
397			// be deleted, only renamed.
398		// Whatever remains at .old must be the previous build, never the stale junk.
399		// Making the install directory read-only would not be portable (Windows
400		// ignores the mode), so this test only covers the missing-source guard: the
401		// target must still be intact afterwards.
402		// StaleSuffix is appended to the previous binary while it is swapped.
403	// Apply replaces target with the freshly downloaded dest.
404	// On Windows the running executable cannot be overwritten but can be renamed, so
405	// target first moves to "<name>.old", the download takes its place, and a stale
406	// ".old" from an earlier update is dropped first. Elsewhere a single rename is
407	// atomic. Renames are retried briefly because antivirus scanners and indexers
408	// lock fresh files for a moment.
409		// The previous update may have left its .old behind (a running binary cannot
410		// be deleted), so make room before renaming.
411			// Not fatal: the rename below surfaces the real problem.
412		// The download directory may sit on another filesystem; copy next to the
413		// target and rename from there, which stays atomic.
414	// looksLikeZip reports whether a file starts with a PK zip signature. It is a
415	// last-resort guard: even if some future download path forgot to unpack the
416	// archive, Apply must never install a zip as the running executable.
417	// looksLikeGzip reports whether a file starts with the gzip magic bytes. It
418	// catches a raw .tar.gz/.tgz that reached Apply without being unpacked, which
419	// looksLikeZip would miss and which would otherwise be installed as the binary.
420	// TestFindAssetIsDeterministicForAmbiguousNames is the regression for a bug
421	// where longestAlias walked a map in random order. A name carrying two aliases
422	// of equal length from different keys (".linux." and ".win32." are both seven
423	// bytes) used to match or not match from one call to the next, so the same
424	// release could install different assets in different processes.
425	// TestFindAssetRejectsAmbiguousArchitecture covers equal-length architecture
426	// aliases from different keys. The asset must be rejected for both platforms
427	// rather than picked randomly as a fallback for one of them.
428	// TestLongestAliasRejectsAmbiguousNames checks the shared helper directly so a
429	// future change to classification cannot silently reintroduce map-order luck.
430	// TestFindAssetRejectsForeignArchForUnknownTarget covers a valid Go GOARCH that
431	// is not in archAliases (for example riscv64). An asset that explicitly names a
432	// different architecture must never be accepted as an OS-only fallback, or the
433	// updater can install an amd64 binary on an unsupported machine.
434		// An asset with no architecture in its name remains a valid OS-only fallback.
435	// FindAsset picks the release asset for a platform. It understands the usual
436	// naming schemes: argent_1.2.3_windows_amd64.zip, argent-windows-x64.zip,
437	// argent_1.2.3_linux_arm64.tar.gz. An asset that names only the operating
438	// system is accepted when no architecture-specific file exists; an asset naming
439	// a different architecture never is.
440	// assetInspection is the shared decision behind FindAsset and
441	// DiagnoseAssetMatch. Keeping both on this helper makes the diagnostic unable
442	// to drift from the asset that is actually selected.
443	// inspectAsset classifies one asset for an already-normalized target platform.
444	// normalizeName lowercases a file name and turns separators into dots so that
445	// aliases match on token boundaries: "arg-x86_64.zip" -> ".arg.x86.64.zip."
446	// classify reports whether the name mentions the target platform and whether it
447	// mentions a different one of the same kind. The longest alias wins, so
448	// "x86_64" counts as amd64 and not as 386. A name that mentions two different
449	// platforms with equally long aliases is ambiguous and is always treated as
450	// foreign: guessing would make FindAsset depend on Go's random map iteration
451	// order and could install the wrong architecture.
452			// An unrecognised target has no aliases in the table, but a name that
453			// mentions a *known* platform still names a different one. Treating that
454			// as "no platform named" would let FindAsset fall back to, say, an amd64
455			// build for a riscv64 machine. An explicit mention of the target wins;
456			// otherwise any known alias makes the asset foreign.
457	// longestAlias finds the platform key whose alias matches name with the longest
458	// token boundary needle. Different keys are visited in sorted order so equal
459	// lengths are handled deliberately: when two *different* keys match needles of
460	// the same length, ambiguous is set and callers must reject the asset instead
461	// of letting the random map iteration decide which platform it names.
462				// An equal-length match for the same key is just an alternative
463				// spelling (for example x86_64 and x86-64). A different key means
464				// the asset name names two platforms and cannot be trusted.
465	// isNoise filters out checksum, signature and metadata files.
466	// TestParseChecksumsAcceptsFileNamesWithConsecutiveSpaces is the regression for
467	// a parser that located the separator by searching for "  " anywhere in the
468	// line. A binary-mode manifest produced by `sha256sum -b` uses " *" after the
469	// digest, so a file name containing two consecutive spaces made the search find
470	// that pair inside the name and reject an otherwise valid checksums.txt.
471	// ChecksumEntry is a single line from a checksums manifest. Hash is normalised
472	// to lowercase hex; Name is the file name as it appeared in the manifest,
473	// without a leading "./".
474	// ParseChecksums parses a checksums.txt / sha256sums.txt manifest of the kind
475	// produced by GNU coreutils and most release pipelines. Every non-blank,
476	// non-comment line must use one of the two canonical forms:
477	//	<64-hex-digest>  <file name>   (text mode: two spaces)
478	//	<64-hex-digest> *<file name>   (binary mode: one space, one asterisk)
479	// A leading "./" on the file name is removed and the digest is lowercased.
480	// On the first malformed line parsing stops and an error naming the 1-based
481	// line number is returned. This is a pure function: it performs no I/O and
482	// never mutates its input.
483	// parseChecksumLine validates and decodes one canonical checksums line.
484		// The digest is the first whitespace-delimited token, so the separator starts
485		// at the first space. Locating it there instead of searching for "  " first
486		// matters for binary-mode lines: a file name may legally contain two
487		// consecutive spaces ("<hash> *my  file.zip"), and looking for "  " anywhere
488		// in the line finds the pair inside the name and feeds a bogus digest to
489		// normaliseChecksum. Checking the byte after the first space accepts exactly
490		// the two canonical separators: "  " (text mode) and " *" (binary mode).
491	// MatchedChecksum associates a release asset with the manifest hash that
492	// covers it.
493	// ChecksumDiagnosis is the read-only result of matching a checksums manifest
494	// against a release's assets. All slices preserve first-seen input order so
495	// identical inputs always produce identical output.
496	// DiagnoseChecksums matches parsed manifest entries against release assets.
497	// Names are compared case-insensitively and the first asset with a given name
498	// wins. An entry whose name carries more than one distinct hash is a conflict:
499	// it is reported in Conflicts and treated as Missing for the assets it names,
500	// because no single hash can be trusted to verify that download.
501	// The function is pure: it neither downloads anything nor mutates its inputs,
502	// so it can be used to decide whether a published checksums file is complete
503	// and trustworthy before any asset is fetched.
504	// TestDiagnoseAssetMatchRejectsForeignArchForUnknownTarget keeps the diagnostic
505	// in step with FindAsset when the target architecture is not in archAliases.
506	// TestDiagnoseAssetMatchAmbiguousName agrees with FindAsset: an asset naming two
507	// platforms with equal-length aliases is foreign, never a silent fallback.
508	// AssetMatchReason is a stable, machine-comparable explanation of how an asset
509	// was classified for a target platform.
510		// AssetMatchReasonSelected marks the single asset FindAsset would pick.
511		// AssetMatchReasonEligible is a usable asset that lost on score.
512		// AssetMatchReasonNoURL means the asset cannot be downloaded.
513		// AssetMatchReasonNoise means the asset is a checksum, signature or metadata file.
514		// AssetMatchReasonWrongOS means the asset names another (or no) operating system.
515		// AssetMatchReasonWrongArch means the asset names a different architecture.
516		// AssetMatchReasonInvalidTarget means goos or goarch was empty.
517	// AssetMatchDiagnosis is the read-only verdict for one asset. The booleans
518	// report the checks FindAsset performs; Reason collapses them into one label.
519	// DiagnoseAssetMatch explains every asset in the same order it was given. The
520	// asset FindAsset would select is marked Selected; ties are resolved the same
521	// way (first wins). Unlike FindAsset it never substitutes runtime.GOOS or
522	// runtime.GOARCH, so identical arguments always produce identical output.
523	// TestWithDownloadDeadline checks the deadline policy without any I/O: a
524	// context without a deadline gets the updater default, while a caller-supplied
525	// deadline is never replaced.
526	// TestDownloadAppliesANetworkTimeout is the end-to-end regression: a server
527	// that accepts the connection and then stalls must not hang the updater, and a
528	// caller deadline must still win. Everything uses local mock HTTP servers.
529	// MaxAssetSize caps a release download (512 MiB) so a hostile or broken
530	// response cannot fill the disk.
531	// downloadTimeout bounds a self-update download when the caller's context has
532	// no deadline of its own. Check uses a 30-second client timeout; downloads need
533	// the same protection, otherwise a server that accepts the connection but never
534	// sends (or stops mid-body) leaves the updater hanging forever. It is a variable
535	// so tests can shrink it without waiting; production always uses 30 seconds.
536	// withDownloadDeadline gives ctx a deadline if it does not already have one.
537	// An existing, possibly shorter, caller deadline is always preserved.
538	// Progress is called after every chunk with the bytes written so far and the
539	// expected total (0 when the server did not report one).
540	// Download fetches asset into dest and verifies its SHA-256 checksum before the
541	// file appears at dest. A checksum mismatch removes the partial download. For a
542	// zip or gzip-compressed tar asset, the verified archive is unpacked with
543	// restricted extraction and the whitelisted executable (argent/argent.exe) is
544	// placed at dest; a bare binary is written unchanged.
545	// DownloadProgress is Download with a progress callback.
546			// The published checksum covers the archive, so it has been verified
547			// above. Extract the executable into a private staging directory and
548			// place that at dest; installing the archive itself would brick the
549			// binary. FindAsset prefers .tar.gz/.tgz on non-Windows platforms, so
550			// those must be unpacked here too (not staged as a raw gzip file).
551	// normaliseChecksum accepts a bare hex digest or "sha256:<hex>".
552	// zipFixture builds an in-memory zip archive from the given entries.
553	// TestZipAssetIsExtractedBeforeApply is the end-to-end regression test: the
554	// published windows archive contains argent.exe, FindAsset picks it, and the
555	// installed binary must be the executable inside the zip, never the zip itself.
556	// TestDownloadRejectsUnsafeZipEntries covers zip-slip, absolute paths, Windows
557	// drive letters and symlinks. Each must fail before anything is staged.
558	// tarGzFixture builds an in-memory gzip-compressed tar archive from the given
559	// entries. A zero typeflag means a regular file.
560	// TestTarGzAssetIsExtractedBeforeApply is the regression for the cross-package
561	// gap: FindAsset prefers .tar.gz over .zip on Linux, so Download must unpack it
562	// rather than stage a raw gzip archive that Apply would install as the binary.
563	// TestDownloadRejectsUnsafeTarEntries covers path escapes, links and duplicate
564	// executables. Each must fail before anything is staged.
565	// TestDownloadRejectsTarGzWithoutAnExecutable also exercises the .tgz suffix.
566		// maxArchiveEntries caps how many entries we inspect in a downloaded zip.
567		// A release archive only carries a couple of binaries.
568		// maxExtractedEntry caps a single uncompressed entry (512 MiB).
569		// maxExtractedTotal caps the sum of all extracted bytes (1 GiB), so a
570		// compressed bomb cannot inflate past the download cap.
571	// executableNames are the only zip entries eligible for installation. The
572	// release archive also ships argent-native.exe, which must never replace the
573	// running CLI binary.
574	// isZipAsset reports whether the asset name denotes a zip archive.
575	// isTarGzAsset reports whether the asset name denotes a gzip-compressed tar
576	// archive. FindAsset gives .tar.gz/.tgz the highest extension bonus on non-Windows
577	// platforms, so Download must unpack them just like zip archives or it would
578	// stage a raw archive and brick the binary.
579	// extractExecutable extracts the whitelisted argent executable from a zip
580	// archive into dir and returns the path of the extracted file.
581	// Extraction is deliberately restricted: absolute paths, ".." traversal,
582	// symlinks and other non-regular entries are rejected; entry counts and byte
583	// totals are capped; and only argent/argent.exe are ever written.
584		// chosenName is a plain base name from safeArchivePath, so it cannot escape
585		// the extraction directory.
586	// extractExecutableFromTarGz extracts the whitelisted argent executable from a
587	// gzip-compressed tar archive into dir and returns the path of the extracted
588	// file. It applies the same restrictions as extractExecutable: path and symlink
589	// checks, entry/byte caps, and a single whitelisted executable. The decompressed
590	// stream is bounded as a whole so a compression bomb cannot inflate past the
591	// per-entry caps regardless of what the tar headers claim.
592		// tar.Reader is a forward-only stream, so bound the total decompressed bytes
593		// rather than trusting individual header sizes when skipping entries.
594				// A regular file; checked against the whitelist below.
595				// Symlinks, hardlinks, devices, FIFOs, etc. must never be installed.
596			// base is a plain name returned by safeArchivePath, so it cannot escape
597			// the extraction directory.
598	// safeArchivePath validates an archive entry name, normalising Windows
599	// separators, and returns its cleaned, slash-separated path. It rejects
600	// absolute paths, drive letters, and any path that escapes its parent.
601	// maxSummaryChanges bounds how many change items SummarizeReleaseNotes prints.
602	// changelogLabels are recognised (case-insensitively, as substrings) as
603	// changelog section headings in a release body. Chinese labels are included
604	// because argent's release notes are bilingual.
605	// NoteChecksum is a SHA-256 digest found in release notes together with the
606	// file name following it. Hash is always lowercase hex; Name is empty for a
607	// bare digest line.
608	// ReleaseNotes is the structured view of a GitHub release body returned by
609		// Summary is the first prose line of the body with inline markdown
610		// stripped. Headings, bullets and checksum lines are skipped.
611		// Changes are the bullet items of the changelog section. If the body has
612		// no recognisable changelog heading, all bullet items are returned.
613		// Checksums are all SHA-256 lines found anywhere in the body, in order.
614	// Notes parses r.Body into structured release notes. It never fails: an empty
615	// or unusual body simply yields empty fields.
616	// ParseReleaseNotes extracts a short summary, a changelog and SHA-256 lines
617	// from a GitHub release body. It is a pure function: no I/O, no mutation, and
618	// identical input always yields identical output.
619	// Headings may use English or Chinese labels (for example "## What's Changed"
620	// may be fenced in code blocks, prefixed by a list marker, or bare digests.
621	// A sub-heading inside a changelog section keeps its bullets in that section;
622	// a heading of the same or higher level ends it.
623	// SummarizeReleaseNotes renders notes as a compact, deterministic,
624	// newline-separated summary suitable for an update prompt or log line. At most
625	// maxSummaryChanges change items are printed; any remainder is counted.
626	// markdownHeading recognises an ATX heading ("## Foo"). It returns the level
627	// (1..6), the heading text, and whether the line was a heading at all.
628	// markdownBullet strips a bullet or ordered-list marker from a trimmed line and
629	// removes inline markdown so the item reads as plain text.
630	// stripListMarker removes a leading "-", "*", "+" or "1." / "1)" marker and
631	// reports whether one was present.
632	// parseNoteChecksum finds a leading 64-character SHA-256 digest on a line,
633	// optionally after a list marker and/or inside backticks, and returns the
634	// lowercased digest plus the following file name (which may be empty).
635		// A longer hex string is not a checksum line; its first 64 characters are
636		// just part of a single (invalid) token.
637	// stripInlineMarkdown removes the common inline markdown decorations so a
638	// summary line reads as plain text.
639	// maxNoticeSummaryRunes bounds the release-note excerpt appended to an update
640	// notice so the whole message stays a compact, single line.
641	// placeholderVersion replaces a version string that fails basic validation, so
642	// untrusted release metadata cannot inject control characters or markup into a
643	// notice shown to the user.
644	// FormatUpdateNotice returns a sanitised, single-line, Chinese-language notice
645	// telling the user that a newer build is available and how to upgrade. It is a
646	// pure function: no I/O, no global state, and identical inputs always produce
647	// identical output.
648	// Both version strings are checked against a conservative ASCII whitelist;
649	// anything else (control characters, spaces, ANSI escapes, markup) becomes a
650	// "?" placeholder. When notes is non-empty a short single-line summary is
651	// appended and capped at maxNoticeSummaryRunes runes (rune-safe for CJK text).
652	// If latest is not newer than current, a short "already up to date" notice is
653	// returned instead.
654	// safeVersionToken returns text unchanged when it is a plausible version tag
655	// made only of ASCII letters, digits, ".", "-", "+" or "_". Anything else is
656	// replaced by placeholderVersion so release metadata cannot inject content.
657				// allowed version character
658	// noticeSummary extracts a short, single-line excerpt from raw release notes.
659	// It reuses ParseReleaseNotes to pick the first prose line (with inline markdown
660	// stripped), falling back to the first change item, then normalises whitespace
661	// and truncates by rune count so CJK characters are never split.
662	// singleLine collapses every run of whitespace or control characters (including
663	// newlines and ANSI escapes) into one ASCII space, so the result contains no
664	// line breaks or terminal control sequences.
665	// truncateRunes shortens text to at most max runes, trimming a trailing space
666	// and appending an ellipsis when it was cut. It is safe for multi-byte runes.
667			// Numeric prerelease identifiers wider than int must compare by value.
668	// Package update checks GitHub releases for a newer argent build, downloads the
669	// right asset with a checksum, and replaces the running binary atomically.
670	// Only the standard library is used: release metadata comes from
671	// /releases/latest, assets are plain HTTP downloads, and the swap is a rename.
672	// apiBase is a variable so tests (and GitHub Enterprise installs) can point the
673	// client elsewhere.
674	// Asset is one downloadable release file.
675	// Release is the subset of the GitHub release payload argent uses.
676	// Version is the tag without its leading "v".
677	// Check fetches the latest release of repo ("owner/name") and reports whether it
678	// is newer than currentVersion. A nil client uses a 30-second default.
679	// Version is a semantic version. Build metadata is dropped at parse time, so
680	// v1.2.3+build.5 and v1.2.3 compare equal.
681		// Pre holds the prerelease identifiers; empty for a final release.
682	// ParseVersion accepts "1.2.3", "v1.2.3", "1.2", and prerelease suffixes such
683	// as "1.0.0-beta.2". Build metadata after "+" is ignored. Numeric prerelease
684	// identifiers with leading zeroes (for example "1.0.0-01") are rejected.
685				// SemVer 2.0.0 §9: numeric identifiers must not include leading zeroes.
686	// Compare orders two versions by the semver precedence rules: a release is
687	// higher than any of its prereleases, numeric identifiers compare numerically,
688	// and a shorter identifier list sorts lower.
689	// isNumericIdentifier reports whether identifier consists solely of ASCII
690	// digits. Such identifiers compare by numeric value, not lexicographically.
691			// Numeric identifiers always have lower precedence.
692	// compareNumericIdentifier orders numeric identifiers by value without going
693	// through int, so identifiers wider than the machine word compare correctly.
694	// Leading zeroes (invalid at parse time, but possible on a hand-built Version)
695	// are stripped first.
696	// String renders the version without the v prefix or build metadata.
697	// CompareVersions compares two version strings. An unparsable string is treated
698	// as 0.0.0 so a broken local version still sees published releases.
699		// The in-memory view is redacted too.
700		// SetDir repoints an existing bank at another directory.
701		// The relevant sentence must lead even though it is not the first in the
702		// memory: overlap outweighs the small early-sentence bonus.
703		// The cap lands inside the text, but the combining sequence and emoji that
704		// fit must be emitted whole, never sliced in half.
705	// Persistent fact store. Unlike the instruction files in memory.go (which are
706	// project-owned, immutable input to the system prompt), this bank is written by
707	// the agent itself and survives across sessions. It is a single JSONL log:
708	// one Memory per line, appended and synced per write, rewritten through a
709	// temporary file for cap enforcement and deletion.
710		// memoryFileName is the append-only log backing a Bank.
711		// defaultCap bounds retained entries: 2000 * 4KB is roughly 8MB on disk.
712		// maxTextBytes bounds the stored text of one entry (and of source/tags).
713		// maxTagBytes bounds a single tag so a malicious model cannot pad the log.
714		// defaultRecallLimit applies when a caller passes a non-positive limit.
715		// tagMatchBonus outweighs a single token hit so explicit tags act as a
716		// strong filter rather than a faint relevance hint.
717		// scannerBufferSize accommodates a 4KB text plus tags and metadata.
718		// maxReflectBytes caps the total size of an extractive reflection, header
719		// included. 4KB keeps the synthesis well inside a model's context budget.
720		// reflectPositionBonus is the small head start given to sentences that
721		// appear early in recall order. It keeps relevance (token overlap) as the
722		// primary ranking signal while preserving a readable, lead-first summary.
723		// skillDirName is the managed subdirectory under the bank dir where
724		// promoted lessons are written as SKILL.md-compatible markdown.
725	// ErrEmptyText is returned when Retain is asked to store nothing.
726	// Memory is one durable fact. TS is stored in UTC.
727		// Invalidated, when non-empty, holds the reason a fact was invalidated and
728		// excludes it from Recall and Reflect. omitempty means normal records never
729		// carry the field, so existing logs and callers are byte-for-byte unchanged
730		// when invalidation is unused.
731	// Bank is a concurrency-safe persistent fact store backed by one JSONL file.
732	// Construct it with NewBank; the zero value is not usable.
733	// NewBank returns a bank rooted at dir, creating the directory when possible.
734	// An empty dir falls back to a private directory under os.TempDir().
735	// SetDir repoints the bank at a new directory. It is intended for setup, before
736	// concurrent use; the file itself is created lazily on the next write.
737	// Dir reports the directory currently backing the bank.
738	// Retain stores a fact and returns its generated id. The text, tags and source
739	// are redacted with security.Redact before anything reaches disk, so the memory
740	// file never contains credentials. Stores over the cap drop the oldest entries.
741			// Keep the tail: the log is in insertion order, so the last cap entries
742			// are the newest ones regardless of clock resolution.
743	// Recall loads the bank and returns up to limit memories ranked by token
744	// overlap with query plus tag matches, ties broken newest-first. A non-positive
745	// limit uses defaultRecallLimit. With an empty query and no tags every memory
746	// is eligible, so the result is simply the newest first. Malformed lines are
747	// skipped leniently.
748			// Invalidated facts are tombstones: kept on disk for provenance but
749			// never surfaced by search. Normal records never carry the field, so
750			// this filter is a no-op for banks that never call Invalidate.
751	// Forget removes the memory with id. It is idempotent: an unknown id is a no-op.
752	// All returns every valid memory in insertion order, skipping malformed lines.
753	// Invalidated memories remain here (and on disk) as tombstones; only Recall and
754	// Reflect hide them.
755	// Reflect produces a deterministic, EXTRACTIVE synthesis over the memories most
756	// relevant to query. It performs NO model call and uses only the standard
757	// library. Unlike plain concatenation, it is a real extractive synthesizer:
758	//  1. Recall returns the top `limit` memories (relevance first, then recency).
759	//  2. Each memory is split into sentences at .!?。！？ boundaries (decimal dots
760	//     such as "3.14" are left alone, and CJK text is kept intact).
761	//  3. Every sentence is scored by case-insensitive token overlap with the query
762	//     plus a small bonus for appearing early in recall order.
763	//  4. Near-identical sentences are deduplicated, keeping the first (best) copy.
764	//  5. The best `limit` sentences are emitted, ranked by score then original
765	//     order, one per "- " line under a header, capped at maxReflectBytes. The
766	//     byte cap is applied with the grapheme-safe clipper so combining marks,
767	//     emoji and flags are never cut in half.
768	// Output is fully stable for a given bank and query; no randomness and no model
769	// call are involved. A bank with no eligible sentences returns a short notice.
770		// Unique query tokens, lowercased; duplicates in the query must not inflate
771		// a sentence's score.
772				// Earlier sentences get a small, bounded head start.
773			// Fold embedded newlines so one sentence stays one reflected line.
774			// Defensive fallback: if no whole cluster fits, the rune-safe clipper
775			// produces the same (empty or rune-aligned) result.
776	// dedupeKey normalises a sentence into a comparison key so near-identical texts
777	// (differing only in case, whitespace, CJK spacing or punctuation) collapse to a
778	// single reflected line. Each token is NUL-delimited so "ab c" and "a bc" do
779	// not collide.
780	// splitSentences splits text at .!?。！？ boundaries, keeping the terminating
781	// punctuation attached to its sentence and absorbing runs of it (e.g. "?!").
782	// A "." between two ASCII digits is treated as a decimal point, not a boundary.
783	// reflectTokens lowercases text and splits it into comparison tokens. Latin and
784	// other non-CJK letters/digits stay whole ("quick," -> "quick"), while Han,
785	// Hiragana, Katakana and Hangul are emitted one rune at a time so a CJK query
786	// can overlap a sentence without whitespace-delimited words. Punctuation and
787	// symbols are dropped.
788	// Learn retains a reusable lesson and, when promote is true, also writes a
789	// lightweight SKILL.md-compatible markdown file under <bank dir>/skills/<id>.md.
790	// It deliberately does NOT import internal/skills (layering): the file simply
791	// follows the frontmatter + body format the skills loader already understands.
792	// Skill writing is best-effort — a failure still leaves the lesson retained and
793	// returns the id with an empty path plus a wrapped error, so a lesson is never
794	// lost just because its optional skill file could not be written.
795	// writeSkill emits one SKILL.md-compatible document for a promoted lesson.
796	// 0600 is best-effort on Windows, where POSIX file modes are largely ignored;
797	// the explicit mode documents intent and is correct on Unix.
798		// Mirror the stored record: use the same redaction + cap Retain applied so a
799		// credential can never reach the skill file through the raw argument.
800	// skillDescription builds a single-line frontmatter description from tags or,
801	// when none are present, a clipped first line of the lesson. Newlines are
802	// flattened so the value cannot break the YAML-ish frontmatter block.
803	// Invalidate marks the memory with id as invalidated, recording reason, and
804	// removes it from Recall and Reflect. The record stays on disk (and in All) as
805	// a tombstone so provenance is preserved. Implementation: set the omitempty
806	// `invalidated` field on the record via the same atomic rewrite used by Forget.
807	// It is idempotent: an unknown id is a no-op.
808	// appendLocked writes one record with a single write + Sync so a crash cannot
809	// leave a partial line in the log. The caller holds b.mu.
810	// loadLocked reads the whole log. Bad lines are skipped rather than failing the
811	// whole read, so one corrupt record cannot hide the rest. The caller holds b.mu.
812	// writeAllLocked replaces the log atomically from a temporary file in the same
813	// directory. Windows may refuse to rename over an existing file, so that case
814	// falls back to removing the target first. The caller holds b.mu.
815	// boundedText trims, redacts and caps a stored string. Redaction happens before
816	// truncation so a clipped credential can never leave a fragment on disk.
817	// cleanTags normalises, redacts and deduplicates tags.
818	// tokenize lowercases text into comparison tokens. Punctuation is removed so
819	// "quick," and "quick" match. CJK text has no whitespace-delimited words, so
820	// never overlap a Chinese sentence, and Reflect would have nothing to synthesise.
821	// newID combines a nanosecond timestamp with random bytes so ids stay unique
822	// even when many facts are retained in the same tick.
823	// checkBOMDoc asserts one loader result against the content budget. A leading
824	// BOM is metadata: the cap applies to the content bytes left after it is
825	// removed, not to the on-disk bytes that include it.
826	// TestBOMDoesNotConsumePerFileByteBudget covers LoadIn and LoadWithBoundary,
827	// the two loaders with a configurable per-file cap. Before the fix the cap was

866	// Package memory — project-root boundary, override files and trust gate.
867	// This file adds an opt-in instruction loader that follows Codex's
868	// instruction-discovery refinement: walk up to the nearest project-root marker,
869	// collect AGENTS.md from the root down to the working directory (root first),
870	// prefer AGENTS.override.md over AGENTS.md in each directory, and skip ALL

872	// The per-file and total caps, rune-safe clipping and truncation marker are not

874	// memory.go directly (same package) and reuses DefaultLimit, totalBudget and
875	// DefaultMarkers are the project-root markers recognised by ProjectRoot when
876	// the caller does not supply its own list. ".git" covers both a directory and
877	// a worktree/submodule pointer file; ".argent" covers agent-native projects.
878	// LoadOptions configures LoadWithBoundary. Zero values are valid: they select
879	// the default markers, the current user's home directory and DefaultLimit.
880		// Markers are the project-root markers passed to ProjectRoot. An empty
881		// slice selects DefaultMarkers.

883		// An empty string means os.UserHomeDir(). Tests set it to a scratch dir.
884		// Limit is the per-file byte cap. Values <= 0 select DefaultLimit; the
885		// 32 KiB total budget always applies.

887	// the rendered system prompt can show provenance. It is intentionally a
888	// separate type from Doc to keep the opt-in loader's contract explicit.
889		// Source is the absolute path of the file this instruction came from.
890		// Text is the file content, possibly clipped at the size limits.
891		// Truncated reports whether Text was cut by either the per-file cap or the
892		// combined total budget.
893	// ProjectRoot walks up from dir and returns the nearest ancestor directory
894	// that contains any project-root marker. It recognises both a ".git"
895	// directory and a ".git" file (worktree/submodule pointer): only the entry's
896	// existence matters. The walk stops at the filesystem root or at home, so a
897	// marker above the user's home directory is never selected. When markers is
898	// empty, DefaultMarkers are used. It returns "" when no marker is found.
899	// projectRoot is the testable core of ProjectRoot: home is supplied explicitly
900	// so tests can control the upper bound without touching the real home dir.
901	// LoadWithBoundary is the opt-in loader with a project-root boundary and a
902	// trust gate.
903	// When trust is false, NO project documents are collected: only user-level
904	// documents from opts.Home (os.UserHomeDir when empty) are returned.
905	// When trust is true, the project root is found with ProjectRoot and
906	// instruction files are collected from that root down to dir inclusive, in
907	// root-to-cwd order. In each directory AGENTS.override.md wins over AGENTS.md
908	// (exactly one of them is used), followed by CLAUDE.md. If no project root is
909	// found, dir itself is used as the single scanned directory.
910	// Files are read and charged against the shared total budget in cwd-to-root
911	// (nearest-first) order, so when the budget runs out the outermost, most
912	// general instructions are the ones dropped and the nearest, most specific
913	// ones survive. This matches the "nearer wins" contract of Block/LoadIn. The
914	// returned slice is still assembled root-to-cwd for callers.
915	// The same DefaultLimit per-file cap, 32 KiB total budget, rune-safe clipping
916	// and TruncationMarker as Load/LoadIn apply. Each Instruction carries its
917	// Source path. Existing Load/LoadIn behaviour is unchanged.
918			// Untrusted: never read project files; only user-level docs.
919			// No project root: scan the working directory only.

921		// budget is spent on the most specific instructions first: when it runs out
922		// the outermost, most general files are the ones dropped. Collect one group

927	// the instructions inside each group (AGENTS slot before CLAUDE.md) in place.
928	// dirsFromRootToDir returns the directory chain from root down to dir,

930	// root is reached (or the filesystem root, as a safety net).

941	// RelevantInstructions returns the subset of docs whose instruction directory

944	// somewhere below D; rules in a sibling or ancestor-of-D subtree do not apply.
945	// This is the path-scoped companion to LoadWithBoundary: instead of injecting
946	// every nested AGENTS.md/CLAUDE.md into the system prompt for the whole run,
947	// a caller can keep the full set resident and pass only the relevant slice to
948	// InstructionsToDocs + Block for the file a tool is about to touch. It is meant
949	// for on-demand injection, which matters when the project has many nested
950	// instruction files but each tool call touches a single subtree.
951	// Source paths are absolute, as produced by LoadWithBoundary. touchedPath may
952	// be absolute (the common case for a workspace-resolved tool path) or relative,
953	// in which case it is resolved against the process working directory via

957	// returns a fresh slice (nil when nothing matches or the inputs are empty).
958	// InstructionsToDocs converts opt-in loader results to the Doc type accepted
959	// by Block, so the app can render them with the existing system-prompt block.
960	// buildBudgetChain writes one AGENTS.md per directory along a chain rooted at
961	// base. sizes are nearest-first: sizes[0] lives in the deepest directory,
962	// sizes[len-1] in base. It returns the deepest directory (the "cwd").
963	// budgetMarkerCases places the walk's remaining budget in the narrow window
964	// [DefaultLimit, DefaultLimit+len(TruncationMarker)) before a file whose body is
965	// exactly DefaultLimit bytes and which was clipped by the per-file cap. Such a
966	// document gets a TruncationMarker appended; the marker must be reserved from
967	// the budget, otherwise the combined text silently exceeds totalBudget. The
968	// last case is a control where the marker still fits.
969	// The chain built by budgetMarkerChain is (nearest first): three over-cap docs
970	// (each DefaultLimit+marker bytes), one small untrimmed "filler" doc, then a
971	// final over-cap doc. The filler size is chosen so that, just before the final
972	// doc is charged, the remaining budget equals tc.remaining.
973	// budgetMarkerChain builds a five-directory chain whose nearest-first file
974	// sizes drive a capped document into the marker window described above.
975		// Three over-cap docs cost 3*(DefaultLimit+marker); the filler then brings
976		// the used total to totalBudget-remaining.
977	// Empty, nil and all-skipped inputs return a nil result, mirroring the loader.
978	// The three required fields — original pattern, semantic description and the
979	// sensitive-directory risk flag — plus glob/negation normalization are checked
980	// for a mix of literal, glob, negated and CJK patterns.
981	// Multiple lines keep input order; skipped lines do not produce entries.
982	// A leading BOM is encoding metadata: it must not become part of a pattern and
983	// a BOM-prefixed comment must still be skipped, matching the file loader.
984	// The function is pure: it never mutates the input and returns a fresh slice.
985	// The reported Glob is exactly the string Ignored matches against: a flagged
986	// sensitive pattern really must exclude its probe path when loaded.
987	// No rules means nothing is excluded, including for a nil ruleset.
988	// A parent's rule applies below it; a child's later rule wins, and `!`
989	// re-includes what an earlier pattern excluded.
990		// Parent rules were collected first, so child rules come last.
991	// A `!` re-includes what an earlier pattern excluded; a final plain pattern
992	// excludes it again. The last matching line always decides.
993	// Comments, blank lines and whitespace-only lines are not patterns; a lone
994	// `!` is discarded rather than becoming a match-all re-include.
995	// Glob semantics come from security.GlobMatch: `*`, `**` and `?`.
996	// Paths are normalized with filepath.ToSlash; native separators, leading `./`
997	// and leading `/` all resolve to the same path.
998	// GlobMatch lowercases both sides, so ignore matching is case-insensitive on
999	// every platform (the same rule the security policy uses).
1000	// A UTF-8 BOM at the start of a .argentignore file is encoding metadata, not
1001	// part of the first line. Without stripping it the first pattern becomes
1002	// "\ufeff*.log" and never matches; worse, a BOM-prefixed comment is mistaken
1003	// for a pattern instead of being skipped. The instruction-file loader already
1004	// strips a leading BOM; ignore files must do the same.
1005	// Invalid input is an error; an entry named .argentignore that is a directory
1006	// is simply skipped, keeping discovery best-effort.
1007	// Hierarchical, advisory .argentignore discovery.
1008	// Unlike the instruction files in memory.go (which are injected into the
1009	// prompt), these files only say what a caller may choose to leave OUT of the
1010	// prompt. This mirrors Cursor's .cursorignore: it is a best-effort convenience
1011	// for keeping obviously irrelevant paths out of context, and is explicitly
1012	// NOT a security boundary. Never use IgnoreRules.Ignored to decide whether
1013	// untrusted content may be read or executed — use internal/security for that.
1014	// IgnoreFileName is the per-directory exclusion file discovered by LoadIgnore.
1015	// maxIgnoreFileBytes caps how much of one .argentignore file is read. Ignore
1016	// files are advisory, so an oversized file is skipped rather than failing the
1017	// whole walk.
1018	// pattern is one parsed .argentignore line.
1019	// Matching reuses security.GlobMatch: memory already depends on
1020	// internal/security (see bank.go), so there is no import cycle, and reusing
1021	// it keeps glob semantics identical to the policy matcher (`*` inside a
1022	// segment, `**` across segments, `?` for one character, plus tail matching).
1023	// Only gitignore-style line handling (`#`, `!`, blank lines) is added here.
1024	// IgnoreRules is the flattened set of .argentignore patterns discovered by
1025	// LoadIgnore, ordered parent directory first so the last matching pattern
1026	// (the most specific one) wins.
1027	// Best-effort and advisory, like Cursor's .cursorignore: NOT a security
1028	// boundary. A false negative only means extra text reaches the context.
1029	// LoadIgnore walks up from dir collecting .argentignore files. Discovery goes
1030	// child -> parent, but the rules are stored parent -> child so a nested
1031	// directory can override an ancestor with `!`.
1032	// Each line is one glob; blank lines and lines starting with `#` are ignored,
1033	// and a leading `!` re-includes a path an earlier pattern excluded. A trailing
1034	// `/` is dropped so directory patterns such as `build/` also match their
1035	// contents. Patterns are normalized to forward-slash globs before matching.
1036	// Only an invalid dir (stat/absolute-path failure) is an error. Unreadable,
1037	// oversized or misnamed entries are skipped: discovery is best-effort and
1038	// must never break prompt construction.

1040		// maxDepth is the same guard used by memory.go for upward walks.
1041	// normalizeIgnoreGlob converts a trimmed .argentignore line into the canonical
1042	// glob form shared by the loader and the diagnostics. .argentignore is
1043	// gitignore-style, where `/` is the separator on every platform, so backslashes
1044	// are normalized unconditionally: filepath.ToSlash only converts the *native*
1045	// separator and would leave `\` intact on Linux (where it is a legal filename
1046	// byte). security.GlobMatch likewise turns `\` into `/` on both sides, so this
1047	// keeps the stored glob identical to what matching actually uses everywhere.
1048	// parseIgnorePatterns turns one file's text into ordered patterns.

1070		// SensitiveRisk reports whether an exclusion pattern may accidentally also

1077	// set mixes common secret/metadata locations with CJK names so the check also
1078	// exercises non-ASCII paths. This is a best-effort warning, NOT a security

1093	// describePattern builds one IgnoreRuleInfo. line is the already BOM-trimmed,
1094	// whitespace-trimmed input; raw is kept verbatim for the Pattern field.
1095		// Only exclusions can "harm" a path by leaving it out of the context.
