1098	// describeGlobSyntax explains which glob metacharacters a pattern uses.
1099	// TestHugeFileIsNotFullyRead guards the streaming read: with a small cap the
1100	// loader must not allocate memory proportional to a very large file.
1101	// TestTotalBudgetCapsCombinedText checks that many capped files cannot push an
1102	// unbounded amount of text into the system prompt.
1103	// TestTruncationIsRuneSafe checks that clipping never splits a UTF-8 rune,
1104	// including CJK and emoji sequences.
1105	// TestNoLimitKeepsWholeFile preserves the documented LoadLimit(limit<=0)
1106	// single-file "no cap" semantics: the file is returned whole and unmarked.
1107	// Package memory loads project instruction files for the system prompt.
1108	// It looks for AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md from the working directory upwards,
1109	// stopping at the user's home directory (or the filesystem root when the
1110	// working directory is outside it). Files are ordered nearest first: a nested
1111	// project file refines the outer one, and the renderer says so explicitly.
1112	// Each file is capped, because a real CLAUDE.md can be thousands of lines that
1113	// would otherwise be re-sent on every turn. Truncation is reported so the model
1114	// knows it can read the file for the rest. Files are read incrementally (at
1115	// most one byte past the cap, plus a leading BOM that is stripped before the
1116	// cap is applied) and the combined text is bounded by totalBudget, so a huge or
1117	// deep tree cannot inflate the system prompt or memory use.
1118	// FileNames are the instruction files read in each directory, in order.
1119	// DefaultLimit is the number of bytes kept from each file.
1120	// totalBudget caps the combined size of the text kept from all files, so a
1121	// deep tree cannot accumulate dozens of capped copies in the system prompt.
1122	// maxDepth stops a walk that never reaches the home directory.
1123	// TruncationMarker is appended to a shortened document.
1124	// Doc is one instruction file found on the way up.
1125		// Path is the absolute path of the file.
1126		// Text is the file content, possibly truncated.
1127		// Truncated reports whether Text was cut at the size limit.
1128	// Load walks up from root, collecting AGENTS.md and CLAUDE.md, and stops at the
1129	// user's home directory. Each file is capped at DefaultLimit bytes.
1130	// LoadLimit is Load with a custom per-file cap. A limit <= 0 means no cap: in
1131	// that mode neither the per-file cap nor the global total budget is applied.
1132	// LoadIn is Load with an explicit home directory, which is also the walk
1133	// boundary. Tests use it to keep the walk inside a scratch tree.
1134					// A per-file clip appends TruncationMarker below. Reserve those
1135					// bytes now: otherwise a capped body that just fits remaining
1136					// can push the combined text past totalBudget once the marker
1137					// is added. Re-clipping to remaining-marker keeps the doc and
1138					// makes the budget exact.
1139	// bom is the UTF-8 byte order mark. When present at the start of a file it is
1140	// encoding metadata, not instruction content, so it must not consume any of the
1141	// per-file byte budget.
1142	// readInstructionFile reads path and returns its trimmed text. When limit > 0
1143	// it reads at most limit+1+len(bom) bytes: the extra byte only tells us whether
1144	// the file is longer, and the BOM bytes are read as well so a leading BOM can be
1145	// removed before the cap is applied (otherwise it would silently eat three
1146	// bytes of content). The whole file is never loaded. The returned text is
1147	// already cut on a rune boundary. With limit <= 0 the entire file is read and
1148	// truncated is always false (the "no cap" contract of LoadLimit).
1149	// clipRunes shortens s to at most limit bytes without splitting a UTF-8 rune.
1150	// The caller guarantees len(s) > limit, so s[limit] exists and is used to
1151	// detect a rune that was cut in half; bytes are dropped back to its start.
1152	// Block renders docs as the block injected into the system prompt.
1153	// The header states the ordering rule: entries run from the most specific
1154	// (nearest) file to the least specific, so a conflict is resolved in favour of
1155	// the first one. Empty input renders nothing.
1156		// "src/parser" appears twice to exercise dedupe of active directories;
1157		// its ancestors are shared by both chains and must appear only once.
1158		// Include a valid dir; the outside ones must be ignored, not fail the call.
1159	// candidate is one discovered instruction file with its rank inputs.
1160	// distance is the number of directory levels between the file and the active
1161	// directory whose walk found it; seq preserves discovery order for ties.
1162	// LoadForDirs collects instruction files for a set of active subdirectories
1163	// and their ancestors, stopping at the workspace root. It is meant for
1164	// "nested AGENTS.md" discovery: when work touches src/parser and src/lexer,
1165	// the caller passes those directories so the specific rules of each subtree
1166	// (and the shared rules above them) are included in the system prompt.
1167	// The input dirs are resolved against cwd. For each dir, every ancestor up to
1168	// and including the workspace root is considered, in nearest-first order: a
1169	// file in a deeper directory outranks the same-named file nearer the root.
1170	// Both FileNames are read in each directory. Paths are deduplicated by their
1171	// resolved (absolute, cleaned, case-folded on Windows) location so a file
1172	// shared by several subtrees is injected only once, at its most specific rank.
1173	// Existing Load behaviour is unchanged; LoadForDirs is an additive API. The
1174	// same per-file limit and total byte budget used by LoadIn apply here, and the
1175	// returned docs are already clipped and ranked for Block(). A directory that
1176	// cannot be resolved or read is skipped rather than failing the whole call.
1177		// Rank lists for each distinct ancestor chain. Shorter distance to the
1178		// active directory means more specific; ties keep the first-seen order.
1179				// Never walk outside the workspace root.
1180			// Reserve the truncation marker's bytes when the per-file cap already
1181			// clipped this body; see the matching logic in LoadIn.
1182	// withinWorkspace reports whether path is the workspace root or lies below it.
1183	// The comparison is done on cleaned absolute paths and is case-insensitive on
1184	// Windows, matching samePath used by LoadIn.
1185	// normalizePath returns a canonical key for deduplication. Windows paths are
1186	// case-insensitive, so fold them; other platforms keep their exact case.
1187	// samePath compares two cleaned paths, case-insensitively on Windows.
1188	// sortCandidates orders docs most-specific first: smaller distance (closer to
1189	// an active directory) wins, then earlier discovery order breaks ties, then
1190	// path text for a stable result set.
1191		// The input slice must be untouched.
1192		// The result must be a fresh slice (no aliasing back to the input).
1193		// All sources empty: result is nil, not an empty non-nil slice.
1194	// TestOrderInstructionsCaseInsensitiveDedupOnWindows guards the documented
1195	// case-insensitive deduplication on Windows; it is a no-op elsewhere.
1196	// OrderInstructions returns docs reordered for cascade-style injection:
1197	// root-to-cwd by the directory depth of each Source path, so instructions
1198	// closest to the working directory come last and, under a "later wins"
1199	// renderer, override the more general rules above them.
1200	// It is the pure ordering companion to LoadWithBoundary/RelevantInstructions:
1201	// it performs no disk I/O, never mutates docs, and returns a fresh slice (nil
1202	// for empty input). The ordering rules are:
1203	//   - Rank by directory depth: a deeper Source directory is nearer to cwd and
1204	//     is placed later. Depth is the number of separators in the cleaned
1205	//     directory part, so the ranking reflects real nesting as long as all
1206	//     sources share a workspace root (as loader-produced docs do).
1207	//   - Same-depth entries are sorted by their normalized Source path text,
1208	//     with a stable tie-break, for deterministic output.
1209	//   - Duplicate sources (by the normalizePath key used elsewhere in this
1210	//     package, case-folded on Windows) are removed, keeping the first
1211	//     occurrence in input order.
1212	//   - Instructions with an empty/blank Source are dropped: they have no path
1213	//     to rank, matching RelevantInstructions' handling of missing sources.
1214	// This is deliberately the opposite end of the ordering spectrum from Block,
1215	// which documents "most specific first, earlier wins". OrderInstructions does
1216	// not change Block or any loader; it is an additive helper for callers whose
1217	// renderer uses a "last wins" convention.
1218	// pathDepth returns the number of directory separators in the cleaned path's
1219	// directory part. Deeper directories (closer to a working directory beneath a
1220	// shared root) yield a larger number, which OrderInstructions uses to place
1221	// nearer instructions later.
1222	// countClusters walks the cluster boundaries produced by the package. It is
1223	// used only for the "never exceeds the cap" property; concrete expected strings
1224	// below are written out literally so a bug in clusterEnd cannot hide there.
1225		// L + V + T jamo form one syllable cluster.
1226	// Grapheme-cluster-safe clipping, implemented with the standard library only.
1227	// A "grapheme cluster" is what a human reads as one character. Splitting a
1228	// string at an arbitrary byte or rune boundary can break a combining sequence
1229	// ("e" + U+0301), a ZWJ emoji (👨‍👩‍👧‍👦), a regional-indicator flag (🇨🇳) or a
1230	// Hangul jamo sequence into invalid or nonsensical fragments. The clippers in
1231	// this file never cut inside such a cluster.
1232	// Coverage (pragmatic, stdlib-only):
1233	//   - a base rune plus following Unicode combining marks (Mn/Mc/Me),
1234	//     variation selectors (FE00–FE0F), ZWNJ, emoji skin-tone modifiers and
1235	//     emoji tag characters;
1236	//   - ZWJ (U+200D) chains: the rune after a ZWJ is glued into the cluster;
1237	//   - paired regional indicators (U+1F1E6–U+1F1FF) form a single flag cluster;
1238	//   - Hangul jamo sequences grouped by the L/V/T composition rules;
1239	//   - CRLF kept together.
1240	// Honest limitations: this is NOT a complete UAX #29 implementation. It has no
1241	// extended-pictographic / prepend / spacing-mark / Indic virama rules, does not
1242	// implement the full GB9c (Indic conjunct) or GB11 (extended pictographic ZWJ)
1243	// gating, and does not tokenise regional indicators with intervening extenders
1244	// perfectly. It is deliberately conservative: when in doubt it keeps bytes
1245	// together, so it may under-count clusters for exotic scripts but will not emit
1246	// a dangling combining mark or a half emoji. Callers needing full conformance
1247	// should use a dedicated UAX #29 implementation; the stdlib has none.
1248	// ClipGraphemes returns the first maxClusters grapheme clusters of s. The
1249	// result is always a valid UTF-8 string and is never longer than s. Non-positive
1250	// maxClusters yields the empty string.
1251	// TruncateGraphemes behaves like ClipGraphemes but appends "…" when the input
1252	// actually had to be shortened. When s already fits in maxClusters clusters it
1253	// is returned unchanged (no ellipsis is added).
1254	// clipGraphemeBytes is the byte-budget sibling of ClipGraphemes. It returns the
1255	// longest prefix of s that fits within maxBytes bytes without cutting a
1256	// grapheme cluster in half. Reflect uses it to enforce a byte cap while keeping
1257	// combining sequences and emoji whole. Invalid UTF-8 input is trimmed to its
1258	// longest valid prefix.
1259	// clusterPrefixEnd walks clusters from the start of s and stops after either
1260	// maxClusters clusters (when >= 0) or maxBytes bytes (when >= 0). The caller
1261	// supplies exactly one limit. It returns a byte offset that is always a cluster
1262	// boundary (and hence a rune boundary for valid UTF-8 input).
1263	// clusterEnd returns the byte offset just past the grapheme cluster that starts
1264	// at byte offset i. It advances at least one rune, so callers always make
1265	// progress even on malformed UTF-8 (where a bad byte decodes as RuneError/size 1).
1266		// A CRLF pair is one cluster.
1267		// Regional indicators pair up left-to-right to form flags.
1268				// ZWJ glues the following rune (and its own extenders) here.
1269	// isClusterExtend reports whether r attaches to the preceding base rune:
1270	// combining marks, variation selectors, joiners and emoji modifiers.
1271	// jamoClass classifies a rune for the Hangul composition rules (UAX #29 GB6–8).
1272	// hangulGlues reports whether next may join the cluster whose last jamo class
1273	// was prev.
1274	// longestValidPrefix returns the longest prefix of s that is well-formed UTF-8.
1275	// This is a defensive guard for callers that pass malformed input; for valid
1276	// input it returns s unchanged.
1277	// InstructionSources on nil/empty/all-blank input returns nil, and skips blank
1278	// sources within a mixed input (matching RelevantInstructions/OrderInstructions).
1279	// A single instruction has no ancestor in the set, so it is never boundary
1280	// limited; all of its provenance fields are reported correctly.
1281	// The core table: nesting decides BoundaryLimited, depth grows with nesting,
1282	// CJK paths are handled, and same-directory files do not limit each other.
1283			// wantLimited is the expected BoundaryLimited value per entry, aligned
1284			// with in (all inputs keep sources, so lengths match).
1285	// Depth must increase by exactly one per nesting level and agree with
1286	// OrderInstructions' pathDepth metric; CJK directory names count as ordinary
1287	// path components (one separator each).
1288	// InstructionSources is pure: the input slice is untouched and the result is a
1289	// fresh slice whose mutation cannot leak back to the caller's docs.
1290	// TestInstructionSourcesCaseInsensitiveAncestorOnWindows guards the documented
1291	// case-insensitive containment used to detect an ancestor scope on Windows;
1292	// it is a no-op on other platforms.
1293	// Source-provenance diagnostics for instruction documents.
1294	// The loaders (LoadWithBoundary, LoadIn, LoadForDirs) attach a Source path to
1295	// every document but do not expose how that path maps to a scope or to the
1296	// project boundary. This file adds a pure diagnostic view over the already
1297	// loaded documents: it reports, for each instruction, where it came from, how
1298	// deep it is, which directory it scopes, and whether it is confined to a
1299	// subtree ("boundary limited") rather than applying across the whole set.
1300	// Like DescribeIgnoreRules, this is a display/debug aid only: it performs no
1301	// disk I/O, injects nothing into the prompt, and never mutates its input. It is
1302	// additive — no loader and no existing exported function changes.
1303	// SourceInfo is the provenance of one instruction document, produced by
1304	// InstructionSources. It is a pure value intended for UI/debug display.
1305		// Source is the cleaned source path, copied verbatim from the Instruction
1306		// (the loaders produce absolute paths). A doc with an empty/blank Source is
1307		// skipped by InstructionSources and never appears here.
1308		// Name is the base name of the source file: AGENTS.md, CLAUDE.md,
1309		// AGENTS.override.md, etc. It lets a UI show which discovery slot a rule
1310		// came from without parsing the path again.
1311		// Depth is the directory-separator count of the source's directory part,
1312		// the same metric OrderInstructions uses to rank nesting. Larger values
1313		// mean a deeper, more specific source.
1314		// ScopeDir is the directory the instruction applies to: the parent of
1315		// Source. The rule governs this directory and everything below it.
1316		// BoundaryLimited reports whether this instruction is confined to a
1317		// subtree. It is true when another document in the same input set has a
1318		// scope directory that is a STRICT ancestor of ScopeDir — i.e. a more
1319		// general rule exists above it, so this one only applies inside its own
1320		// subtree boundary. A root-level instruction (no ancestor in the set), a
1321		// lone instruction, and instructions sharing the same directory are never
1322		// limited. This is a set-relative diagnostic, not a loader/trust fact: it
1323		// is derived solely from the paths supplied to InstructionSources.
1324	// InstructionSources returns a displayable provenance record for every
1325	// instruction with a non-blank Source, preserving input order. It is the pure
1326	// provenance companion to LoadWithBoundary/RelevantInstructions/OrderInstructions:
1327	// it performs no disk I/O, never mutates docs, and returns a fresh slice (nil
1328	// for nil/empty input or when every source is blank).
1329	// Path handling matches the rest of the package: paths are cleaned, directory
1330	// containment and equality are case-insensitive on Windows (samePath /
1331	// withinWorkspace), and Depth reuses pathDepth so the two diagnostics agree on
1332	// what "deeper" means. When the same directory contributes more than one file
1333	// (AGENTS.md alongside CLAUDE.md), all of them share one ScopeDir and none is
1334	// marked BoundaryLimited relative to the other.
1335			// A strict ancestor scope in the set makes this instruction subtree-
1336			// scoped. Equal directories are excluded so sibling files in one dir
1337			// do not mark each other as limited.
1338	// asyncConfig builds a config with an optional background hook and an optional
1339	// synchronous hook. Both get generous timeouts so only the behaviour under
1340	// test (background execution) can make a run slow.
1341				// Bounded wait so a regression that makes Run wait for the async
1342				// hook fails on elapsed time instead of hanging the test binary.
1343		// Cancelling the parent after Run returns must not cancel the detached hook.
1344	// The dedup and stop-propagation walk in planRun and planAsync must agree on
1345	// which commands are claimed. They currently do not, which produces two bugs:
1346	// the same command string can run in BOTH paths, and an async hook carrying
1347	// StopPropagation does not shield later synchronous hooks.
1348		// Two documents disagree on "async" for the same command. The first
1349		// (highest-priority, here declaration-order) occurrence claims the command;
1350		// the duplicate must not also run in the other path.
1351	// planAsync selects the hooks marked Async for background execution. It draws
1352	// from the same claimedCommands walk as planRun, so a command is claimed once
1353	// across the whole event, a stop boundary shields later hooks in both paths,
1354	// and the background and synchronous sets are disjoint. Async hooks never
1355	// enter planRun's tiers, so the returned decision is unaffected by async work.
1356	// launchAsync starts each background hook and returns without waiting for it.
1357	// The spawned context is detached from the caller's cancellation with
1358	// WithoutCancel: an async hook is fire-and-forget work that should survive the
1359	// request that triggered it, even when that request's context is cancelled as
1360	// soon as Run returns. Values (session ID, call ID, ...) are preserved. The
1361	// hook's own timeout is still applied, so wall-clock time stays bounded.
1362	// Panic containment, telemetry and the runner all come from guardedRunCommand,
1363	// so a background hook is observed exactly like a synchronous one. A final
1364	// recover in the goroutine keeps any unexpected failure from taking down the
1365	// process; Done runs last so WaitAsync always drains.
1366	// WaitAsync blocks until every async hook launched by Run on this dispatcher
1367	// has finished. Run itself never calls it, so async work cannot delay the
1368	// decision. Call it only after Run has returned, when no further hooks can be
1369	// added; tests and graceful-shutdown paths use it to drain background work.
1370	// issueKey is the stable part of a ConfigIssue used for table comparisons;
1371	// Message is checked separately (non-empty, mentions the command).
1372	// Config completeness diagnostics.
1373	// Validate (in hooks.go) inspects the raw JSON document and catches silent
1374	// parse-time typos. This file complements it with a check that no document can
1375	// trigger: Async and StopPropagation are internal-only (json:"-") flags set by
1376	// programmatic callers (built-in hooks), so they are invisible to Validate.
1377	// DiagnoseConfig is pure — it reads nothing, spawns nothing and never mutates
1378	// the config; findings are returned, never logged or panicked.
1379	// IssueKind classifies one completeness problem found in a Config.
1380		// IssueAsyncStopPropagation marks a command that is both Async and
1381		// StopPropagation: a background hook that still ends the claim walk.
1382	// ConfigIssue is one finding from DiagnoseConfig. Index is the 0-based position
1383	// of the offending command in Config.Commands; Event and Command copy its event
1384	// and text so callers can locate the hook without re-reading the config.
1385	// Message is a stable, human-readable explanation suitable for doctor output or
1386	// UI warnings.
1387	// String renders an issue for direct display: "[kind] Event[index]: message".
1388	// DiagnoseConfig reports completeness problems in an already-parsed Config.
1389	// It is a read-only diagnostic: nothing is dispatched or mutated, and a nil or
1390	// empty config yields an empty (non-nil) slice.
1391	// It checks the one dimension Validate cannot reach — the combination of the
1392	// internal-only Async and StopPropagation flags. A command carrying both ends
1393	// claimedCommands' walk and shields every later hook, but Run never observes
1394	// the outcome of an async hook (a deciding hook must answer synchronously).
1395	// The effect is later hooks silently skipped on the strength of a fire-and-
1396	// forget job, which is almost always unintended.
1397	// FormatConfigIssues renders config diagnostics as stable, one-line-per-issue
1398	// text suitable for doctor output or a UI warning. It is a pure display helper:
1399	// no I/O, no mutation, and an empty (or nil) list yields an empty string so a
1400	// caller can omit the whole section when there is nothing to report.
1401	// Order is preserved and every line is ConfigIssue.String(), which carries the
1402	// kind, event, 0-based index and message so a reader can locate the exact
1403	// offending command. CJK command text and the message are copied verbatim.
1404	// TestMain doubles as the hook command under test: when the helper variable is
1405	// set, this process behaves like a tiny hook script instead of a test binary.
1406			// Bounded sleep used by the parallel-execution tests. Given a generous
1407			// hook timeout, N concurrent hooks take ~one sleep, not N sleeps.
1408			// Record one line per invocation so tests can count executions. A
1409			// single small append keeps concurrent writers from interleaving.
1410			// Exit immediately but leave a child holding this process's stdout
1411			// and stderr — the pipes the dispatcher captures. Without a WaitDelay
1412			// the dispatcher's Wait() blocks until the child releases them.
1413			// Like "lingering", but exit only once ARGENT_HOOK_GO_FILE appears so
1414			// the test can place the process exit just before the hook deadline.
1415			// ARGENT_HOOK_READY_FILE tells the test the child is already holding
1416			// the captured pipes.
1417			// Used to obtain a real *os.ProcessState with a known exit status for
1418			// the classification unit tests.
1419			// Hold the inherited pipes until the test signals it is done (with a
1420			// fallback bound so a failed test cannot leak a process forever).
1421	// helperDispatcher runs the test binary instead of a shell.
1422		// Generous timeout: under a full parallel `go test ./...` (or -race) the
1423		// shell can take several seconds just to start. This test checks that the
1424		// shell is used and output captured, not a tight wall-clock bound.
1425		// findBash probes up to five interpreters with a two-second deadline each
1426		// on Windows, and it runs before runCtx exists so its cost is not bounded
1427		// by the hook timeout. A caller that supplies Shell has already chosen the
1428		// interpreter; RunCommand must not probe the default one at all.
1429		// Sanity: without a Shell override the default path must still probe, so
1430		// a zero count above cannot be an artefact of a disabled probe.
1431		// Release the child before this binary exits; on Windows a live child
1432		// would otherwise keep the test executable locked during go test cleanup.
1433		// The helper exits at once but its child inherits the captured stdout and
1434		// stderr pipes and keeps them open. The hook's own timeout must bound the
1435		// wall-clock wait; without WaitDelay the run takes seconds instead of
1436	// dispatchLingering runs a hook that exits just before its deadline while a
1437	// background child keeps the captured pipes open past it. The deadline then
1438	// fires during the post-exit pipe drain, which the old runCtx.Err() check
1439	// misread as a hook timeout. The helper signals when the child is holding the
1440	// pipes so the timing stays deterministic even under -race.
1441		// Warm the one-time bash probe before starting the clock; on Windows its
1442		// first call can spend up to two seconds checking candidate interpreters.
1443		// Place the process exit 400ms before the deadline. It is gone when the
1444		// context expires, so only the postponed pipe drain runs past the deadline.
1445		// The hook process exited well before its deadline; only the post-exit
1446		// pipe wait ran into it. Its real exit status must survive, and it must
1447		// not be mislabelled as a timeout.
1448		// The same pipe-drain overrun must not swallow a real exit-2 block: the
1449		// block signal is only honoured for a hook that did not time out.
1450	// processStateForExit runs the helper just to obtain a real *os.ProcessState
1451	// with a known exit status; the classification tests need a genuine state
1452	// because its fields are unexported.
1453		// A background child holding the captured pipes makes Wait report
1454		// ErrWaitDelay after the deadline, even though the hook already exited.
1455		// The context's status must not overwrite the real exit code.
1456		// and "🙂" is 4 bytes, so 400 and 120 are guaranteed to land mid-rune for
1457		// some repetition counts.
1458		// Strings within the limit are returned unchanged (no stray ellipsis).
1459	// --- parallel dispatch (Crush audit port) -----------------------------------
1460		// Serial execution would take at least 8×500ms = 4s. A generous 2.5s bound
1461		// leaves room for slow process startup under -race / a loaded CI while still
1462		// catching a regression to the old serial loop.
1463			// Order and the block flag survive aggregation regardless of the order
1464			// in which the concurrent hooks finished.
1465		// FileChanged matchers describe the affected file ("*.go"), but the
1466		// convenience wrapper only fills Payload.Path. Matching must look there.
1467		// The path is the documented match value for FileChanged. Even when a
1468		// caller also supplies a Tool, a matcher must be tested against the path.
1469		// planRun receives commands already sorted by descending priority, as Run
1470		// guarantees after its stable sort.
1471		// maxCapture bounds one stream captured from a hook.
1472		// summaryClip bounds the output quoted in a Result.Message.
1473		// waitDelay bounds how long Wait keeps the captured stdout/stderr pipes
1474		// open after the hook process exits. A hook that spawns a background
1475		// process inheriting those pipes would otherwise hold them open past the
1476		// command's timeout, stalling the whole (parallel) dispatch. The delay is
1477		// still long enough for ordinary output to flush.
1478	// Payload is the JSON document written to a hook's stdin.
1479		// CallID optionally identifies the tool call that triggered this hook. It
1480		// is carried into PanicError so a bad callback can be correlated with the
1481		// call that was being dispatched.
1482		// Prompt is the user message for UserPromptSubmit and Stop.
1483		// Output is the tool result for PostToolUse.
1484		// Error is the tool failure for PostToolUseFailure.
1485		// Path is the file affected by a FileChanged event. It is empty for other
1486		// events; callers that mutate several files fire one event per path.
1487		// HookPayload is optional event-specific detail for the turn-end events
1488		// (EventInterrupt, EventStopFailure, EventTurnEnd): the classification
1489		// reason, the last assistant message or the error text. It is empty for
1490		// every other event, so existing payload JSON is unchanged.
1491	// Result is the aggregate decision of one dispatch.
1492	// Blocked is true only when a PreToolUse hook exited with status 2. Message
1493	// carries the blocking hook's output, or the warnings of the hooks that failed
1494	// without blocking.
1495	// Outcome is what one hook command did.
1496	// Message is a one-line summary of the outcome.
1497	// CommandRunner executes one hook command. The zero-value Dispatcher uses
1498	// RunCommand, which spawns a shell. Tests inject a CommandRunner to observe
1499	// execution without touching the process table. A runner must honour ctx's
1500	// cancellation and deadline so the dispatcher's timeout still bounds a hook.
1501	// Dispatcher runs hook commands.
1502		// Timeout bounds one command when the command itself sets none; zero
1503		// means 30 seconds.
1504		// Shell overrides the command interpreter, for tests and odd platforms.
1505		// The default is bash -c (cmd /C on Windows when bash is absent).
1506		// Telemetry, when non-nil, is invoked once per hook executed by
1507		// Dispatch/Run with its elapsed time, exit status and failure reason. It
1508		// runs from the hook's own goroutine while hooks are parallel, so the
1509		// callback must be safe for concurrent use. Leaving it nil (the default)
1510		// adds no telemetry work to a run.
1511		// SlowThreshold marks HookTelemetry.Slow true once a hook's elapsed time
1512		// reaches it. Zero (the default) disables the mark. It never changes
1513		// dispatch or timing; callers decide how to warn on slow records.
1514		// Runner, when non-nil, replaces the built-in process runner for every
1515		// hook, synchronous or asynchronous. It is the injection seam used by
1516		// tests to observe hooks without spawning a real shell.
1517		// asyncWG tracks background hooks launched by Run. Run never waits on it:
1518		// an async hook must not delay the decision that triggered it. WaitAsync
1519		// lets callers drain them (tests, graceful shutdown).
1520	// Dispatch runs every matching hook for payload.Event and returns the
1521	// aggregate decision.
1522	// DispatchDocument runs the hooks of a trusted document.
1523	// FireFileChanged dispatches a FileChanged lifecycle event for one changed
1524	// path. It is a convenience wrapper that fixes the event name and payload
1525	// field, so every caller produces the same hook document shape. Callers that
1526	// need session/cwd context fill those on the app-side bridge, which owns the
1527	// run context, before dispatching.
1528	// Run executes the synchronous hooks for one event, highest Priority first.
1529	// Hooks with equal Priority keep their declaration order (stable sort), so
1530	// document hooks — all at priority 0 — behave exactly as before. Async hooks
1531	// are launched in the background and never enter the returned decision: a
1532	// deciding hook must answer synchronously. A command with StopPropagation ends
1533	// the run after it executes, shielding later hooks.
1534	// Hooks of one priority tier run concurrently; tiers are drained in priority
1535	// order. Aggregate latency drops from O(N×T) to roughly O(T) per tier, while
1536	// the executed set and the order of the returned outcomes stay identical to
1537	// the previous serial loop. StopPropagation is a static flag, so planRun can
1538	// compute the short-circuit boundary before any command starts.
1539		// FileChanged matchers describe the affected file ("*.go"), not the tool
1540		// that changed it. FireFileChanged and other direct callers carry only
1541		// Payload.Path; ForEvent skips matcher-bearing hooks when the match value
1542		// is empty, so without this the documented file matchers never fire.
1543		// Launch background hooks before the synchronous tiers so a slow or
1544		// cancelled tier cannot stop them from starting. Run never waits for them.
1545	// claimedCommands walks the sorted command list once and returns the commands
1546	// that actually execute: duplicate command strings are dropped (the first
1547	// occurrence in priority/declaration order wins) and a StopPropagation command
1548	// ends the walk, shielding every later hook. A duplicate still honours its own
1549	// stop boundary. Both planRun and planAsync derive from this single walk so
1550	// they cannot disagree about which commands were claimed: every command goes
1551	// to exactly one of the synchronous or background paths, and a stop on either
1552	// path shields later hooks in both.
1553	// planRun mirrors the old serial loop's control flow using the claimed set:
1554	// synchronous commands are grouped into priority tiers, each holding
1555	// equal-priority commands that may run concurrently, in execution order.
1556	// resultCollector gathers outcomes from concurrent hook runs. The mutex keeps
1557	// collection race-free; each goroutine records under its original index so
1558	// ordered() can rebuild declaration order regardless of completion order.
1559	// runTier runs one priority tier concurrently. A cancelled context stops new
1560	// hooks from launching; hooks already started finish under their own timeout
1561	// and are still collected. Outcomes are returned in tier declaration order.
1562	// commandRunner returns the runner used for every hook. The zero value keeps
1563	// the original process-spawning behaviour; only tests that need an injection
1564	// seam override it.
1565	// timeoutFor resolves the effective timeout of a command: the command's own
1566	// value, then the dispatcher's, then defaultTimeout, clamped to minTimeout.
1567	// Both the synchronous process path and the background path use it so a hook
1568	// is bounded the same way regardless of how it was launched.
1569	// guardedRunCommand runs one hook with panic containment. The only in-process
1570	// callbacks a hook can panic through are Dispatcher.Shell and Runner; a command
1571	// that panics becomes an Outcome with a PanicError, and the loop still reaches
1572	// the next command. The command string is the fallback identity when the caller
1573	// did not supply a tool call ID.
1574		// Take the clock only when telemetry is on, so the default path pays no
1575		// extra timing cost beyond what RunCommand already does.
1576	// RunCommand runs one hook command with the payload on stdin.
1577		// A caller-supplied Shell has already chosen the interpreter. Resolving
1578		// the default one first would run findBash's probes (up to five processes
1579		// with a two-second deadline each on Windows) even though the result is
1580		// discarded — and it happens before runCtx exists, so that cost is not
1581		// covered by the hook's timeout.
1582		// A timed-out or killed process may leave a grandchild holding the pipe
1583		// write ends; bound how long Wait waits for those pipes so the timeout
1584		// above really bounds wall-clock time.
1585		// The context can expire while Wait is still draining the captured pipes
1586		// after the process itself has exited — a hook that leaves a background
1587		// child holding stdout keeps them open past the deadline. os/exec still
1588		// calls Cancel in that case, but Kill returns ErrProcessDone: the hook did
1589		// not time out. Track only a Kill of a live process so a completed hook is
1590		// not mislabelled as timed out (which would also drop its exit-2 block).
1591		// Exit status 2 is the hook protocol's "block" signal, and only PreToolUse
1592		// may block an action.
1593	// classifyRunResult turns a finished process wait into the status fields of an
1594	// Outcome. killedByDeadline is true only when the timeout actually killed a
1595	// live hook. Without it, a context that expired while Wait was draining pipes
1596	// left over by a background child must not rewrite the process's real exit
1597	// status: the hook already finished, and its exit-2 block must survive.
1598				// ErrWaitDelay and a late context error only mean the captured
1599				// pipes outlived the process; they are not hook failures.
1600	// Summarize turns outcomes into the block/warn decision.
1601	// shellCommand picks the interpreter for a hook command. Hooks are shell
1602	// scripts, so bash is preferred when a working one is installed.
1603	// findBash returns a bash that actually runs. On Windows the first "bash" on
1604	// PATH is often the WSL alias in System32, which fails when no distro is
1605	// installed, so each candidate is probed once.
1606	// bashRuns probes one candidate interpreter. It is a variable so tests can
1607	// count probes without spawning processes.
1608	// limitedBuffer captures at most maxCapture bytes.
1609	// clipRunes bounds text to limit bytes without cutting a UTF-8 rune in half.
1610	// A trailing ellipsis takes the place of the dropped tail. The backoff walks
1611	// from limit-1 to the preceding RuneStart, so the cut always lands on a rune

1961	// for its index with StatusOK; failed and skipped records do not count. The
1962	// function walks forward from current and returns the first index that is not
1963	// completed as resumeFrom. skipCompleted reports whether that walk advanced
1964	// past current (the step at current, and possibly more, was already done and

2027	// isBuiltinPlaceholder reports whether name is one of the runner's reserved

2069	// recorder keeps every prompt and tool call for assertions.
2070	// Package workflow runs scripted multi-step tasks.
2071	// A definition is a small JSON document:
2072	//	{"name":"review","steps":[
2073	//	  {"name":"scan","prompt":"list the changed files"},
2074	//	  {"name":"review","prompt":"review {{steps.scan.output}} for {{input}}"},
2075	//	  {"name":"report","prompt":"write the report","when":{"previous_contains":"BUG"}}
2076	// Steps run in dependency order: a step that reads {{steps.NAME.output}} runs
2077	// after the step that produces it. Each step either prompts the model or calls
2078	// a tool, and can carry a condition on the previous step's output, a timeout,
2079	// retries with exponential backoff, and an on_failure policy.
2080	// Failure policies.
2081		// OnFailureStop ends the run; the remaining steps are recorded as skipped.
2082		// OnFailureContinue keeps going with the next step.
2083		// OnFailureRetry retries the step (at least once) and stops when the
2084		// attempts are exhausted.
2085	// Duration is a JSON-friendly time.Duration: it marshals as a string ("30s")
2086	// and accepts a duration string or a number of seconds when decoding.
2087	// Std returns the value as a time.Duration.
2088	// String renders the duration the way time.Duration does.
2089	// MarshalJSON writes the duration as a string.
2090	// UnmarshalJSON accepts "45s", 90 (seconds), "" and null.
2091			*d = Duration(v)
2092		*d = Duration(time.Duration(seconds * float64(time.Second)))

2094		// PreviousContains runs the step only if the previous output contains it.
2095		// PreviousNotContains runs the step only if the previous output does not

2097	// matches reports whether the condition holds. prev is the output of the last
2098	// step that actually ran; a skipped step never becomes the previous.

2140	// Summary is the order-independent aggregate of a set of graded Results.

2143		// Total is the number of results handed to AggregateScores.

2145		// Skipped is the number of invalid results ignored (Total - Valid).
2146		// Passed and Failed are counted over valid results only.

2148		// never NaN or +Inf.

2152		// FailureCounts groups failed valid results by their normalized reason.

2155	// failureReasonKeyRunes bounds the length of a FailureCounts key without
2156	// ever cutting a UTF-8 rune in half (see clip for the UTF-8 rationale).
2157	// valid reports whether a Result is structurally sane enough to aggregate.
2158	// Results from Run carry a 1-based Index, a non-negative turn count and a
2159	// non-negative duration; anything else is a malformed record and is ignored.
2160	// AggregateScores folds results into a Summary. It is pure: it never mutates
2161	// results, and it depends only on the multiset of valid results, not on their
2162	// order. Results gathered concurrently may therefore be passed in any order.
2163	// failureReasonKey normalizes a failure reason into a stable, rune-safe map
2164	// key. All Unicode whitespace is removed first so that reasons differing only
2165	// in spacing (common with CJK text) fall into the same bucket; clip then
2166	// truncates by runes, so CJK and emoji are never split mid-rune and the key

2210		// Cross-check: the recorded score really is computed from produced bytes.
2211	// Package eval's edit benchmark measures the REAL built-in edit tool, end to

2228	// policy/approval wiring. The harness constructs a yolo registry so the
2229	// mutating tool runs; a test exercises the yolo=false mutation gate as a
2230	// signature proving the registry path is used. It is fully offline and
2231	// A case "passes" when the observed outcome matches the case's declared

2233	// WantResult asserts a successful edit whose final content contains the needle;
2234	// with neither, any successful edit passes. Failure-expected cases are as

2292	// editSimMaxRunes bounds Levenshtein's DP so a pathological input cannot
2293	// allocate or loop without limit. Inputs at or below the bound score normally;
2294	// anything larger returns the -1 sentinel, which callers label "unbounded" and
2295	// exclude from means.
2296	// Levenshtein returns the rune-level edit distance between a and b, with
2297	// insert, delete and substitution each costing 1. It is symmetric and works on
2298	// runes, so CJK text is never cut in the middle of a character. It returns -1
2299	// when either input exceeds editSimMaxRunes runes; callers must treat -1 as the
2300	// "unbounded" sentinel, never as a distance.
2301		// Keep the DP rows as short as possible: one row per rune of the shorter
2302		// string, regardless of which argument it came from.
2303	// EditSimilarity returns 1 - distance/max(rune lengths) in [0,1]; two empty
2304	// strings score 1. It returns -1 (the unbounded sentinel from Levenshtein)
2305	// when either input exceeds editSimMaxRunes: callers must label that case
2306	// "unbounded" and leave it out of any mean.

2308	// whether the resulting token sequences are identical. Unicode letters (Han
2309	// included) and digits are kept; whitespace and punctuation are ignored. Order
2310	// matters, so "a b" and "b a" are not an exact token match.

2325	// seedExecWorkspace writes a tiny module into a throwaway directory. It mirrors
2326	// the harness' workspace seeding without depending on run.go.
2327			// A leading "/" is rooted on both Windows and Unix: Go accepts "/" as
2328			// a separator on Windows too, and Validate checks it explicitly, so
2329			// these cases are safe to assert on every platform.
2330			// Validate splits on both "/" and "\", so a backslash ".." traversal
2331			// is refused even on Unix where "\" is an ordinary filename character.
2332		// A leading "\" is rooted only on Windows: it resolves against the drive
2333		// of cmd.Dir (the disposable workspace), so Validate must refuse it there.
2334		// On Unix "\" is an ordinary filename character, not a path separator, so
2335		// asserting ErrExecBadPath for these there would be wrong.
2336	// TestExecDeadlineBoundsTheTimeout pins the documented timeout contract: 0
2337	// (and negative) use the 30s default, positives are taken verbatim, and any
2338	// value above 120000 is capped. In particular a very large TimeoutMS must not
2339	// overflow into a negative duration, which would make the deadline fire
2340	// immediately rather than applying the cap.
2341	// TestRunExecCheckHugeTimeoutDoesNotExpireImmediately proves the overflow fix
2342	// end to end: a huge TimeoutMS must behave like the 120s cap, so a fast
2343	// command is graded on its exit code rather than an instantaneous deadline.
2344	// TestRunExecCheckRejectsRootedPath proves a rooted command is refused before
2345	// any process starts. On Windows a leading "\" is rooted against the drive of
2346	// cmd.Dir (the disposable workspace), so "\tool.exe" would otherwise run from
2347	// the drive root — outside the workspace entirely. A leading "/" is rooted on
2348	// every platform and is exercised unconditionally; the backslash forms are
2349	// Windows-only because on Unix "\" is an ordinary filename character, not a
2350	// path separator.
2351		// A real *exec.ExitError is expected here; it must not block a grade that
2352		// explicitly wants this non-zero exit code.
2353		// execOutputCap%3 == 1, so a total payload of execOutputCap+2 bytes made
2354		// mostly of 3-byte runes puts the initial drop (2) on a continuation byte:
2355		// the trim has to advance to the next rune boundary and therefore keeps
2356		// fewer than cap bytes. The old code copied that many bytes but truncated
2357		// to cap, leaving a stale byte at the tail.
2358		// an index divisible by 3; that makes the byte the buggy code would leave
2359		// at cap-1 a UTF-8 rune starter (and hence invalid on its own).
2360		// The trim advances drop from 2 to 3, so the correct tail is payload[3:]
2361		// with nothing appended to it.
2362		// execShellMeta lists command separators, redirections, substitutions and
2363		// control characters. No shell is used here, so these are rejected as
2364		// defence in depth: a future refactor must not accidentally make them live.
2365	// Sentinel errors so callers can classify a rejected fixture with errors.Is.
2366	// ExecCheck grades a scenario by running an allowlisted command inside the
2367	// seeded workspace. It is strictly opt-in: nothing in the eval harness invokes
2368	// RunExecCheck on its own, so a fixture must name one explicitly.
2369	// Security model: this is a LOCAL, DETERMINISTIC harness, NOT a sandbox. There
2370	// is no OS isolation, no network restriction and no user switching. Safety
2371	// comes from four bounds:
2372	//  1. Validate must accept the command (go test/build or a relative .exe).
2373	//  2. No shell is involved; argv is passed straight to os/exec.
2374	//  3. Dir is always the disposable temp workspace, never the real repo.
2375	//  4. The process is killed after a short, capped timeout.
2376	// Treat a fixture that enables an exec check as trusted code, exactly like the
2377	// Go test files a repository already runs. This is meant for pass@1-by-running
2378	// on local, deterministic fixtures, not for grading untrusted submissions.
2379		// Command is either "go" or a fixture-local relative path ending in .exe.
2380		// Args is passed verbatim to the process; for "go" it must start with
2381		// "test" or "build".
2382		// TimeoutMS is the deadline in milliseconds: 0 means 30s, values above
2383		// 120000 are capped at 120s.
2384		// WantExitCode is the exit status that counts as a pass.
2385		// WantStdoutContains, when set, must appear in the captured stdout tail.
2386	// Validate rejects any command that is not explicitly allowlisted. It is
2387	// called again by RunExecCheck, so a malformed check cannot bypass the bounds
2388	// even if a caller forgets to validate first.
2389	// validateFixtureExe accepts only a relative path to a fixture-local .exe.
2390	// Absolute paths (including Windows drive-relative ones), ".." traversal and
2391	// unknown bare commands such as sh/bash/curl are all refused.
2392		// filepath.IsAbs alone misses a rooted-but-driveless Windows path such as
2393		// "\tool.exe". Windows resolves that against the *drive of cmd.Dir*, and
2394		// RunExecCheck sets cmd.Dir to the disposable workspace, so the command
2395		// would actually run from the drive root (e.g. D:\tool.exe) — outside the
2396		// workspace the four bounds promise to stay inside. Reject a leading
2397		// separator explicitly; on Unix a leading "/" is already absolute, but
2398		// checking both spellings keeps the rule identical on every platform.
2399	// validateExecArgs screens argv. Shell metacharacters are rejected even though
2400	// no shell is used; -exec/-toolexec are refused because they let `go test` or
2401	// `go build` launch an arbitrary binary, defeating the command allowlist.
2402	// ExecResult is the observed outcome of one exec check. StdoutTail and
2403	// StderrTail hold at most the last 64KB of output, prefixed with a marker when
2404	// truncated (the marker is kept on a rune boundary).
2405	// RunExecCheck runs c inside workspace. The workspace is expected to be a
2406	// disposable temp directory seeded by the eval harness; this function never
2407	// changes directories itself and never starts a shell.
2408		// Resolve "go" to an absolute path in the parent's context BEFORE Dir is
2409		// applied. Without this, a seeded go.exe in the workspace could be picked
2410		// up by a relative PATH entry on Windows.
2411				// Start/chdir failure: the command never ran, so never grade it as
2412				// a successful exit 0.
2413	// execDeadline resolves a fixture's TimeoutMS into a bounded wall-clock
2414	// deadline. Values at or below zero use the default; values above the cap are
2415	// clamped to it.
2416	// The cap is applied in milliseconds BEFORE converting to time.Duration.
2417	// Multiplying a large int by time.Millisecond first can overflow int64 and
2418	// wrap to a negative duration, which would trip the context deadline
2419	// immediately instead of applying the documented 120s cap.
2420	// GradeExecCheck is a pure grading function: exit code must match and, when
2421	// requested, stdout must contain the needle. A timeout or a failure to start
2422	// is always a failure. A non-zero exit is fine when it is what the check wants.
2423	// execEnvironment drops GOFLAGS so a fixture cannot smuggle extra flags (for
2424	// example -toolexec) into the allowlisted go command via the environment.
2425	// tailBuffer keeps only the last execOutputCap bytes written to it. Trimming
2426	// advances to the next rune boundary so a tail never starts mid-UTF-8-rune.
2427			// Advancing to a rune boundary may drop more than execOutputCap would
2428			// on its own, so the surviving tail is shorter than cap.
2429			// Truncate to exactly the bytes copied over. Truncating to cap here
2430			// would leave stale bytes from before the copy at the tail (possibly
2431			// half a rune), which String() would then expose.
2432	// This file adds a golden-snapshot layer on top of the core eval suite. The
2433	// regular suite only asserts the per-scenario expectations in core.json; a
2434	// golden snapshot also freezes the *observable output* of a few stable
2435	// scenarios, so a silent change in the loop's event sequence or final answer
2436	// fails the test instead of slipping through.
2437	// Everything here is test-only: it reuses fakeRun (the scripted loop) and
2438	// runOne (the harness) from the same package, adds no exported symbols and
2439	// needs no real model, network or clock.
2440	// goldenScenarios selects a small, deterministic subset of core.json. None of
2441	// these depend on the network, the clock or randomness: every reply comes from
2442	// the scripted provider and every file is seeded into a scratch workspace.
2443	// The four cover distinct behaviour: a plain answer, a tool result feeding the
2444	// next request, the approval gate, the policy gate and a two-tool/three-turn
2445	// updateGolden reports whether the caller asked to regenerate the snapshots.
2446	// An environment variable is used instead of a flag so `go test` flags stay
2447	// untouched and `go test ./internal/eval` keeps its usual behaviour.

2449	// compares its normalized output with the committed golden file. A mismatch
2450	// prints a line diff. Set ARGENT_UPDATE_GOLDEN=1 to rewrite the snapshots.
2451				// runOne grades the scenario and returns the observable result:
2452				// the tool-event sequence, the final assistant text, turn count,
2453				// stop flag and the pass/reason outcome. It is fully deterministic

2486	// End-to-end orchestration evals.
2487	// Unlike the core suite (whose fakeRun is a hand-written stand-in for the loop),
2488	// this harness drives the REAL agent loop and the REAL task tool. The only
2489	// injected fakes are the things a real harness always injects: a fake model
2490	// (an llm.Chatter, like scripted.go's Scripted) and a no-op ToolRunner for
2491	// non-task tools. Persona resolution, PARENT-RECHECK verification, the bounded
2492	// retry loop, the background queue / drain and the TaskBoard are all the real
2493	// implementations from internal/agent.
2494	// The fake model is *routing* rather than a single global queue: a subagent
2495	// shares its parent's Chatter, so the script must choose replies by participant

2497	// this file reuses its Respond/ToolCall wire types but routes each turn by the

2500	// between the parent's turns.

2599	// callRun turns a panicking runner into a failed scenario instead of a dead
2600	// workspacePath resolves a fixture-declared path inside the scratch workspace.
2601	// Scenario file paths are documented as relative to the workspace, and the
2602	// harness promises that its I/O stays inside the disposable directory that
2603	// runOne removes afterwards. A ".." or absolute path would otherwise escape
2604	// (on Windows filepath.Join turns "../x" into the parent of dir), write or

2617	// limit counts characters (runes), not bytes: the text being clipped is model
2618	// output, seeded file content and exec output, any of which may hold CJK or
2619	// emoji. Slicing a Go string by byte index used to leave a half-written UTF-8
2620	// rune at the cut, which made failure reasons invalid UTF-8. Keep one rune
2621	// fewer and append the ellipsis so the result is still at most limit runes.
2622	// Package eval runs deterministic agent scenarios against a scripted model.
2623	// A unit test proves that a function returns what it should. It does not prove
2624	// that the agent, its tools and its policy still agree with each other after a
2625	// change. That is what an eval is for: a set of scenarios, each with a scripted
2626	// model, run against the real loop and checked against what actually happened.
2627	// The model is scripted, so an eval is deterministic and costs nothing. The
2628	// loop and its tools are real, so a broken tool fails the eval.
2629	//	    "name": "reads a file before answering",
2630	//	    "prompt": "what is in notes.txt?",
2631	//	    "files": {"notes.txt": "the release notes are here\n"},
2632	//	      {"respond": {"tool_calls": [{"name": "read", "arguments": {"path": "notes.txt"}}]}},
2633	//	      {"expect_request_contains": ["release notes"],
2634	//	       "respond": {"text": "notes.txt says the release notes are here"}}
2635	//	    "expect": {"tool_calls": ["read"], "text_contains": ["release notes"], "turns": 2},
2636	//	    "check": {"files_exist": ["notes.txt"], "file_contains": {"notes.txt": "release"}}
2637	// The package does not import the agent: the caller injects a RunFunc, so the
2638	// same scenarios can drive any loop implementation.
2639	// ToolCall is a tool invocation the scripted model asks for.
2640		// Arguments is raw JSON. A JSON string holding JSON ("{\"path\":\"a\"}")
2641		// is accepted too, because scripts are written by hand.
2642	// UnmarshalJSON accepts arguments as either an object or a JSON string.
2643	// ArgumentsString returns the raw arguments, defaulting to an empty object.
2644	// Respond is the message the scripted model returns for one turn.
2645		// Usage is billed usage for this turn, including any cached_tokens count.
2646	// ScriptTurn is one scripted model reply plus an optional expectation about the
2647	// request that triggered it.
2648		// ExpectRequestContains lists substrings that must appear in the
2649		// conversation the model is sent: message text, tool names, tool arguments
2650		// and tool results. It is how a scenario asserts that a tool result really
2651		// made it back into the context.
2652		// ExpectRequestCacheFree fails when any message in the request carries a
2653		// stable-prefix cache marker (the prompt-cache opt-out case).
2654		// ExpectRequestCached fails when no message carries a cache marker.
2655	// Expect is what a scenario expects to have happened.
2656		// ToolCalls is the exact tool sequence, in order. nil means "do not
2657		// check"; an empty (non-nil) list means "no tool may run".
2658		// TextContains lists substrings the final answer must contain.
2659		// TextNotContains lists substrings the final answer must not contain.
2660		// Turns is the expected number of model turns, counted by the harness from
2661		// the scripted provider, not reported by the loop.
2662		// Stopped says whether the run was stopped by something other than the
2663		// model finishing: a turn limit, a policy block, a cancellation.
2664		// CachedTokens is the expected total of cached prompt tokens reported by
2665		// the scripted provider. nil means "do not check".
2666	// Check inspects the workspace after the run.

2668		// FileContains maps a path to a needle its content must contain.

2672		// default-off: an Exec without AllowExec is never run, so a scenario
2673		// document cannot execute a command unless it asks for it by name.
2674	// Scenario is one scripted eval case.
2675		// Model and System are passed to the loop as-is; Model defaults to
2676		// Provider is the script the fake model replays, one entry per turn.
2677		// Files seeds the workspace before the run, path → content.
2678		// The fields below are hints for the caller's RunFunc. The harness itself
2679		// only enforces Repeat (through the scripted provider); the rest describe
2680		// what the scenario wants the loop to do, so a runner does not have to
2681		// special-case scenarios by name.
2682		// MaxTurns is the turn budget for the run (0 = the loop's default).
2683		// CancelAfter asks the runner to cancel the run after that many turns.
2684		// Approve is the answer the approval gate should give (nil = runner's
2685		// DenyPaths lists paths the policy must refuse.
2686		// Repeat plays the script in a loop, so the model never stops on its own
2687		// and only a limit ends the run.

2689		// runner assembles a cache-capable request.

2691		// markers, even when the protocol supports them.
2692	// DefaultModel is the model id handed to the loop when a scenario is silent.
2693	// Load reads a scenario document from disk.
2694	// Parse decodes a scenario document: {"scenarios": [...]} or a bare array.
2695		// Validate after normalising so error messages can name the scenario,
2696		// including the ones that inherited a generated name.
2697	// normalize applies the defaults a hand-written document may omit.
2698	// Scripted is an llm.Chatter that replays a scenario's script instead of

2705	// Chat returns the next scripted turn.
2706			// A real gateway always answers; an exhausted script says so instead
2707			// of hanging the loop.

2720	// TestToolNamesAreNormalizedBeforeDispatch covers a boundary the existing
2721	// validation only checks halfway: validateTurn accepts a tool name with
2722	// surrounding whitespace (it checks strings.TrimSpace(call.Name)), but the
2723	// scripted providers forwarded the raw name to the tool runner. The real tool
2724	// registry does an exact map lookup, so " read " became "unknown tool" at run
2725	// time even though the document was accepted as valid. The canonical name is
2726	// the trimmed one; both providers must dispatch that same value validation
2727		// End to end: fakeTools only knows "ls", so an untrimmed name would come
2728		// back as an unknown tool and the expected tool sequence would not match.

2734		// Emulate what Parse does before validating.

2742	// ErrInvalidScript marks a scenario whose scripted provider cannot be replayed
2743	// as written. Callers can classify load-time failures with errors.Is.
2744	// Validate checks the scenario's scripted model turns before a run starts.
2745	// Parse (and therefore Load) calls it, so a typo in a hand-written document is
2746	// reported with the scenario name and turn number instead of surfacing later
2747	// as a confusing tool error or a desynchronised script.
2748	// validateTurn checks one ScriptTurn. A turn is invalid when it cannot produce
2749	// a usable model reply, or when its two cache expectations contradict each
2750	// other and can never both hold.
2751			// ArgumentsString already unwraps a JSON string holding JSON and
2752			// defaults a missing value to {}. Everything else must be an object:
2753			// the agent's tool runner decodes arguments into a struct.
2754	// Client-side errors.
2755		// ErrClosed reports a client whose server has exited or been closed.
2756		// ErrNoCommand reports a server specification without a program to run.
2757		// ErrTimeout reports a diagnostics wait that expired before a publish.
2758	// Server describes how to launch one language server.
2759		// Name identifies the server in messages, e.g. "gopls".
2760		// Command is the program, resolved through PATH.
2761		// Args are passed to the program.
2762		// Env holds extra KEY=VALUE pairs layered over the current environment.
2763		// LanguageIDs are the LSP language ids this server owns.
2764		// RootURI overrides the workspace URI derived from the root path.
2765	// Client is one running language server and the documents it knows about.
2766		// mark is the publish sequence at the time of the last didOpen/didChange,
2767		// used to tell new diagnostics apart from ones the caller already saw.
2768	// inbound is one decoded JSON-RPC message, whatever its kind.
2769	// Start launches the server for root, completes the initialize handshake, and
2770	// returns a ready client. The context bounds the handshake only; the client
2771	// keeps running until Close.
2772	// newClient wires a client onto an already connected transport. Tests use it
2773	// with an io.Pipe pair; Start uses it with a child process.
2774	// Root returns the absolute workspace root the client was started for.
2775	// ServerName returns the specification's name.
2776	// initialize performs the LSP handshake.
2777					// Advertise the IDE-grade features implemented in features_ide.go
2778					// and features_advanced.go so servers actually send results.
2779	// call sends a request and waits for the matching response.
2780	// notify sends a notification, which has no response.
2781	// cancelRequest asks the server to drop an abandoned request.
2782	// readLoop turns framed messages into responses, notifications and answers.
2783	// dispatch routes one decoded message.
2784			// A server may print banners on stdout; ignore unparsable frames.
2785	// answer replies to a server-initiated request so the server never blocks.
2786			// We have no settings to offer; an empty array is the documented answer.
2787	// handleNotification keeps the state the tools read.
2788	// finish records the terminal error and wakes every waiter exactly once.
2789	// Close shuts the server down: shutdown, exit, then kill as a backstop.
2790			// Closing stdin under the write lock keeps it from racing a straggler.
2791			// Give the server a moment to exit on its own; killing a process that
2792			// is already leaving turns a clean exit into a spurious failure.
2793	// drainStderr keeps the tail of the server's stderr for diagnostics.
2794	// tailBuffer keeps the last n lines of a stream.
2795	// publishPayload builds a textDocument/publishDiagnostics body with a single
2796	// diagnostic carrying message. A nil version emulates a server that omits the
2797	// optional version field.
2798	// diagClient returns just enough client state to exercise storeDiagnostics
2799	// without starting a transport.
2800		// A delayed publish for the previous version lands after the change.
2801		// The caller's mark still points at the change; the current result must win.
2802	// DidOpen announces a document with its current text.
2803	// DidChange replaces a document's content and bumps its version. An unknown
2804	// document is opened first, because servers reject changes for closed files.
2805	// DidSave reports that the document was written to disk.
2806	// DidClose tells the server the document is no longer tracked.
2807	// Version reports the version last sent for a document, or 0 if unknown.
2808	// LanguageID reports the language id a document was opened with.
2809	// Open reads a file, sends didOpen (or didChange when already open) and
2810	// returns its URI.
2811	// Refresh re-reads a file from disk and sends didChange.
2812	// publishParams is the textDocument/publishDiagnostics payload.
2813	// storeDiagnostics replaces the diagnosis for one URI and wakes its waiters.
2814		// LSP allows a server to publish diagnostics for an older document version
2815		// after a newer one (delayed or out-of-order publishes). Such a result is
2816		// stale: accepting it would bump seq past the caller's edit mark and make
2817		// Diagnostics hand back old diagnostics as if they were current. Drop it
2818		// without touching seq, the cache or any waiter. Servers that omit the
2819		// version are unaffected: their publishes keep the original behaviour.
2820	// Diagnostics returns the diagnostics for uri.
2821	// It returns immediately when the server has already published newer
2822	// diagnostics than the caller's last edit. Otherwise it waits up to wait for
2823	// the next publish, and honours ctx. A timeout is reported as ErrTimeout;
2824	// context cancellation as the context error. In both cases any diagnostics
2825	// that did arrive are returned alongside the error.
2826		// A publish may have landed between the first check and registration.
2827	// DiagnosticsAll returns every diagnostic the server currently reports.
