3041	// URIToPath turns a file:// URI back into a filesystem path.
3042		// /C:/x is the URI form of C:\x.
3043	// Package auth stores provider credentials and runs OAuth 2.0 logins without
3044	// third-party SDKs.
3045	// Tokens live in a single JSON file inside a directory (mode 0600, written
3046	// atomically). Two login flows are supported: the browser authorization-code
3047	// flow with PKCE (RFC 7636) and the device-code flow (RFC 8628). Provider
3048	// presets supply the endpoints; the package itself only knows the standards.
3049	// Token kinds.
3050		// KindOAuth marks a token obtained from an OAuth flow.
3051		// KindAPIKey marks a manually pasted API key.
3052	// expirySafety refreshes a token slightly before the provider's own deadline,
3053	// mirroring the Rust implementation's 60-second margin.
3054	// Token is one stored credential.
3055		// Kind is KindOAuth or KindAPIKey.
3056		// Access is the bearer secret: an OAuth access token or an API key.
3057		// Refresh is the OAuth refresh token, empty for API keys.
3058		// ExpiresAt is the access-token deadline; the zero value means "no expiry".
3059		// Account optionally records which account the token belongs to.
3060	// Bearer returns the secret to send to the provider.
3061	// Status describes how usable a stored token is right now.
3062	// Status values reported by Store.Status.
3063	// String implements fmt.Stringer.
3064	// expirySkew is the window in which a still-valid token is reported as
3065	// expiring, so callers can refresh ahead of time.
3066		// Feed each decision's Wait back in: two slow_down responses lengthen the
3067		// interval twice, which is how DevicePoll keeps backing off.
3068	// DevicePollState is the state the device-code polling loop moves into after a
3069	// single token-endpoint answer. It makes the RFC 8628 polling state machine
3070	// visible to callers instead of hiding it inside DevicePoll's loop.
3071	// DevicePollState values reported by ClassifyDevicePoll.
3072		// DevicePollAuthorized means the endpoint returned a token; polling is done.
3073		// DevicePollWaiting means the user has not finished yet; poll again after Wait.
3074		// DevicePollExpired means the device code (or the polling window) ran out.
3075		// DevicePollDenied means the user declined the authorization.
3076		// DevicePollFailed means an unexpected response; stop without retrying.
3077	// String implements fmt.Stringer.
3078	// DevicePollDecision is one step of the device-code polling state machine.
3079	// Wait is the delay before the next poll and is meaningful only when State is
3080	// DevicePollWaiting; for every terminal state it is zero.
3081	// ClassifyDevicePoll maps one token-endpoint result into the next polling
3082	// decision. It is a pure function: no I/O, no clock, no sleeps, so callers can
3083	// drive the state machine in tests or reuse the classification independently of
3084	// A nil err is success (the response already carries the token). A tokenError
3085	// with code authorization_pending keeps the loop waiting at the same interval;
3086	// slow_down also keeps it waiting but lengthens the interval by
3087	// deviceSlowDownStep; expired_token and access_denied are terminal. Any other
3088	// error (including a non-tokenError transport failure) is a failure that stops
3089	// the loop. The supplied interval is passed through unchanged except on
3090	// slow_down, so a caller can feed the previous decision's Wait back in.
3091	// stubDeviceSleep replaces the polling wait so tests never sleep for real.
3092	// deviceScript answers each token poll with the next scripted body.
3093				// The deadline is derived from this first reading.
3094	// TestDevicePollSendsClientSecretWhenConfigured covers the device-grant token
3095	// request: a confidential client must authenticate its polls too.
3096	// containsAll asserts every fragment occurs in a request body.
3097	// DeviceAuth is the result of starting an RFC 8628 device-code flow.
3098		// Interval is the polling interval in seconds.
3099		// ExpiresIn is the device code lifetime in seconds.
3100	// ErrDeviceExpired reports that the device code (or the polling window) ran out.
3101	// ErrDeviceDenied reports that the user declined the authorization.
3102	// deviceSleep and deviceNow are variables so tests can run the polling loop
3103	// without real waits.
3104	// DeviceStart asks the provider for a device code and a user code.
3105	// DevicePoll polls the token endpoint until the user approves, declines or the
3106	// code expires. It honours authorization_pending and slow_down.
3107			// DevicePollWaiting: adopt the (possibly lengthened) interval, then
3108			// wait before the next poll.
3109	// sleepContext waits for d but gives up as soon as ctx is cancelled.
3110	// expiringFixture builds one store covering every classification the scanner
3111	// must distinguish. now is fixed so each token's state is unambiguous.
3112		// Put rejects a secretless token, but a manually edited file can contain
3113		// one; the scanner must skip it rather than report an unusable credential.
3114	// ExpiringTokens lists the provider IDs whose stored credential is due to
3115	// expire within the next `within` window, including credentials that have
3116	// already expired. It is a read-only diagnostic aid: the result contains only
3117	// provider IDs, never an access or refresh secret, so it is safe to log, show
3118	// in a UI, or drop into a support bundle.
3119	// A credential counts as expiring while its remaining lifetime is at most
3120	// `within`; a non-positive `within` therefore narrows the scan to credentials
3121	// already past their deadline at `now`. A negative `within` behaves like zero.
3122	// Entries with no deadline (API keys) and entries with no secret are skipped,
3123	// since they never expire. `now` is explicit so callers and tests share a
3124	// clock, and classification reuses TokenStatus so the margin and overflow
3125	// rules stay identical across the package.
3126	// Results follow the store's provider order. If the store cannot be read,
3127	// ExpiringTokens returns nil rather than a silently empty list, so a corrupt
3128	// credential file is never mistaken for a clean bill of health.
3129	// TestAuthorizeURLDoesNotLetExtraOverrideSecurityParameters guards the
3130	// precedence between provider extras and the OAuth protocol parameters. An
3131	// Extra entry named "state" or "code_challenge" must not replace the values
3132	// passed to AuthorizeURL: Callback.Wait validates against the former and
3133	// Exchange proves possession of the verifier for the latter.
3134		// The 60-second safety margin is applied to expires_in.
3135	// TestExchangeKeepsShortLivedTokensUsable guards the expiry-safety margin: a
3136	// credential whose lifetime is shorter than the safety window must not be born
3137	// expired. The margin exists to trigger an early refresh, not to discard an
3138	// otherwise valid token the moment it is issued.
3139	// TestTokenRequestsSendClientSecretWhenConfigured guards the confidential-client
3140	// path: a Config with a ClientSecret must authenticate every token-endpoint
3141	// request, while public PKCE clients must not emit an empty client_secret.
3142	// TestCallbackRejectsRedirectWithoutCode guards the callback's success path: a
3143	// redirect that carries the expected state but no code and no error must be
3144	// reported as a failure, never as a successful login with an empty code.
3145	// TestCallbackCloseCancelsAPendingWait guards the contract in Close's doc:
3146	// closing the callback must unblock a Wait even when its context is never
3147	// cancelled. Closing only the TCP listener leaves the HTTP server idle and the
3148	// Wait stuck on its select forever.
3149		// A second Close must not panic (the closed channel may only be closed
3150		// once) and must not report a new error.
3151		// WaitCallbackState with a zero port cannot report the chosen address to the
3152		// caller, so bind explicitly to exercise the redirect path.
3153		// Guards against silently renaming the wire fields the Rust store uses.
3154	// Config describes one provider's OAuth endpoints and client identity. The
3155	// presets in this package fill it in; a custom provider may set the fields
3156		// Provider is the id stored with the token (for example "openai").
3157		// ClientID is the public OAuth client identifier.
3158		// ClientSecret is only needed by providers that are not public clients.
3159		// AuthorizeURL is the browser authorization endpoint.
3160		// TokenURL exchanges codes and refresh tokens.
3161		// DeviceURL starts a device-code flow (RFC 8628).
3162		// RedirectURI must match the loopback listener used for the callback.
3163		// Scopes are joined with spaces in requests.
3164		// Audience is an optional extra authorize parameter (Auth0 style).
3165		// Extra adds provider-specific authorize parameters.
3166		// HTTPClient overrides the default client (useful in tests).
3167	// PKCE returns a fresh RFC 7636 verifier and its S256 challenge.
3168	// S256 is the base64url-encoded SHA-256 of verifier, the code_challenge value.
3169	// ValidatePKCE reports whether verifier produces challenge under method, using
3170	// the code_challenge_method values defined by RFC 7636 section 4.2:
3171	//   - "S256": challenge must equal BASE64URL-ENCODE(SHA256(ASCII(verifier))),
3172	//     i.e. the value returned by S256.
3173	//   - "plain": challenge must equal verifier verbatim. An empty method is
3174	//     treated as "plain", which is the RFC 7636 default when the parameter is
3175	//     absent from a request.
3176	// Any other method is unknown and yields false, even if the strings would
3177	// match under a known method. The verifier/challenge comparison is
3178	// constant-time. ValidatePKCE is a pure function: it performs no I/O and never
3179	// panics, so callers can use it to diagnose a broken PKCE pair before sending
3180	// it to a provider.
3181	// AuthorizeURL builds the browser URL for the authorization-code flow.
3182		// Provider extras are applied first so they can contribute optional
3183		// parameters (prompt, access_type, ...), but the protocol-critical values
3184		// set below always win. Letting an Extra entry override `state` would break
3185		// the CSRF check in Callback.Wait; overriding the PKCE challenge or method
3186		// would make the verifier sent by Exchange fail at the token endpoint.
3187	// tokenError is a structured OAuth error response. Device polling inspects the
3188	// code, so the endpoint status travels with the message.
3189	// Exchange swaps an authorization code for tokens.
3190	// Refresh exchanges a refresh token for a new access token. The previous token
3191	// supplies the provider, kind, account and refresh-token fallback.
3192		// Confidential clients authenticate at the token endpoint with their
3193		// secret (RFC 6749 sec 2.3.1). Public PKCE clients leave it empty, so no
3194		// parameter is added for them. Doing this here covers code exchange,
3195		// refresh and the device grant in one place.
3196	// tokenFromResponse converts the wire response into a stored token. expires_in
3197	// is shortened by expirySafety so refresh happens before the provider's own
3198			// The safety margin is a hint to refresh early, not a reason to throw
3199			// away a credential that is still valid. When the provider issues a
3200			// token whose whole lifetime fits inside the margin, clamp the margin
3201			// to half the lifetime so the token remains usable until close to its
3202			// real deadline instead of being reported as expired at issuance.
3203	// ErrStateMismatch is returned when the provider echoes an unexpected state,
3204	// which means the callback did not belong to this login attempt.
3205	// ErrNoAuthorizationCode is returned when the redirect carries the expected
3206	// state but neither an authorization code nor an error. Such a callback cannot
3207	// complete the login and must never be reported as a success.
3208	// ErrCallbackClosed is returned by Wait when Callback.Close is called before
3209	// the redirect arrives. Closing the listener alone cannot unblock Wait, since
3210	// the HTTP server simply stops serving and never delivers a result.
3211	// Callback is a loopback listener that receives one OAuth redirect.
3212		// closed is signalled by Close so a pending Wait can unblock even when the
3213		// caller did not provide a cancellable context.
3214	// Listen binds addr (empty means 127.0.0.1:0) and returns the pending callback.
3215	// Addr is the bound loopback address, for example 127.0.0.1:51234.
3216	// URL is the redirect URI to hand to the provider.
3217	// Wait serves the redirect and returns the authorization code. Every response
3218	// is a small HTML page so the browser shows something friendly. When state is
3219	// non-empty it must match the callback's state parameter.
3220				// A redirect with the right state but no code (and no error) is
3221				// not a completed login. Treat it as a failure so callers never
3222				// receive an empty code together with a nil error, and the browser
3223				// is not told the authorization succeeded when it did not.
3224	// Close releases the listener and cancels a pending Wait. It is safe to call
3225	// more than once; the first call's listener error is returned to the caller.
3226	// WaitCallback binds addr, waits for the redirect and returns the code. It does
3227	// not check the state parameter; use WaitCallbackState when the expected state
3228	// is known (the usual case).
3229	// WaitCallbackState binds addr and returns the code only when the redirect
3230	// carries the expected state.
3231	// Provider is a named login preset. Kind says how a credential is obtained:
3232	// KindAPIKey means the user pastes a key, KindOAuth uses the browser flow and
3233	// the device kind uses RFC 8628.
3234		// Note is a short hint shown by login commands.
3235	// Kind device-code presets report this in Provider.Kind.
3236	// presets is ordered: it is the order login commands should list providers in.
3237	// Presets lists the built-in provider presets in display order.
3238	// Preset looks up a preset by id (case-insensitive).
3239		// Replacement keeps a single entry per provider.
3240		// Deleting a missing provider is not an error.
3241	// fileToken is the on-disk shape: a Unix timestamp keeps zero values out of the
3242	// file instead of serialising year 1.
3243	// Store keeps provider tokens in one JSON file inside a directory.
3244	// Open prepares dir (creating it with mode 0700) and returns a store backed by
3245	// the auth.json file inside it. Nothing is read until the first lookup.
3246	// DefaultDir returns the per-user credential directory, <config>/argent.
3247	// Path is the JSON file backing the store.
3248	// Get returns the token stored for provider.
3249	// List returns every stored token, ordered by provider id.
3250	// Put stores a token, replacing any previous entry for the same provider.
3251	// Delete removes the token for provider and reports whether it existed.
3252	// Status reports whether the provider's token is valid, close to expiry,
3253	// already expired, or absent. now is explicit so callers and tests share one
3254	// save writes the whole document through a temporary file so a crash never
3255	// leaves a half-written credential file behind.
3256		// Rename keeps the temp file's mode; set it again for filesystems that
3257		// ignore modes on create.
3258		// expiresAt-now would wrap negative; the deadline is effectively infinite,
3259		// so it must not be classified as expired or expiring.
3260		// The same deadline moves from valid to expiring to expired as now advances.
3261	// TokenState describes how usable a token deadline is, independent of any
3262	// store or clock. It is the pure counterpart of Status: TokenStatus works on
3263	// raw Unix seconds so callers can classify without loading a credential file
3264	// and can choose their own refresh margin.
3265	// TokenState values reported by TokenStatus.
3266		// TokenUnknown means no deadline is known (the on-disk zero sentinel).
3267	// String implements fmt.Stringer.
3268	// TokenStatus classifies an access-token deadline. expiresAt and now are Unix
3269	// seconds; refreshMargin is the number of seconds before expiry at which a
3270	// still-valid token is considered close enough to warrant a proactive refresh.
3271	// A non-positive expiresAt is the "no expiry" sentinel and yields TokenUnknown.
3272	// A negative refreshMargin is treated as zero (refresh only once expired). The
3273	// comparison is overflow-safe: deadlines far outside the int64 subtraction
3274	// range are reported as TokenValid rather than wrapping into the past.
3275		// expiresAt > here, so a non-positive difference means expiresAt-now
3276		// wrapped: the true deadline is effectively infinite and still healthy.
3277	// tokenRefreshMarkers are the token-invalidity phrases that make a refresh
3278	// worthwhile. Deliberately narrower than the general auth-error classification
3279	// in internal/llm: a 403 or an invalid API key cannot be fixed by refreshing.
3280	// Matching is case-insensitive; CJK markers are unaffected by lowering.
3281	// ShouldRefreshOnError reports whether a provider failure is a token-expiry
3282	// signal worth refreshing for. Only an explicit 401 or an invalid/expired-token
3283	// message qualifies; every other status or message returns false so transient
3284	// or permission errors never trigger a needless refresh. The decision is a pure
3285	// function of its two arguments and visible to callers (transparent).
3286	// TestClaimIsStaleFreshUnstampedMarker captures the window between O_EXCL
3287	// creating a lock file and the owner writing its stamp. The marker is empty
3288	// but brand new; treating it as an abandoned claim lets a second caller steal
3289	// a run that is already in flight.
3290	// TestClaimIsStaleUnusableMarker verifies the documented contract: a marker
3291	// that cannot be read as a regular file must be reclaimable rather than
3292	// wedging the task forever. A directory at the lock path is the portable way
3293	// to make the marker unusable on every platform.
3294	// TestClaimRejectsFreshUnstampedLock is the end-to-end version of the race:
3295	// while one caller owns a just-created lock (before its stamp is written), a
3296	// second caller must get errBusy instead of stealing the claim and running the
3297	// same task twice.
3298		// Simulate the instant right after the first caller's O_EXCL succeeded.
3299	// TestClaimReclaimsAbandonedLock is the positive counterpart: a genuinely old
3300	// claim must still be recoverable so a crashed process cannot wedge a task.
3301	// errBusy reports that an id already has an in-flight run. It is the single
3302	// claim signal shared by the daemon loop and RunNow, so both paths honour the
3303	// same "at most one run per id" rule even in different processes.
3304	// staleClaimAfter is how old a claim must be before a fresh process treats it
3305	// as abandoned. A normal run releases its claim on completion; this only
3306	// recovers from a process that died mid-run. It is deliberately long so a
3307	// slow-but-live task is never duplicated.
3308	// claimDir holds the cross-process in-flight markers and the per-id run
3309	// counters. It lives in the store directory, which every caller of RunNow and
3310	// every daemon shares, even when their log directories differ.
3311	// claimFileName maps an arbitrary (possibly hand-written) task id to a safe,
3312	// stable file name without trusting it as a path component.
3313	// claim atomically reserves id for one run and returns the run's monotonic
3314	// sequence number. The reservation is a file created with O_EXCL, so it is
3315	// visible to the daemon and to RunNow even when they run in separate processes.
3316	// A caller that gets a nil error must call release when its run finishes.
3317			// A crashed process cannot release its claim; reclaim it after the
3318			// grace period so a restarted daemon is not wedged forever.
3319	// release drops the in-flight reservation for id. Releasing an id that was
3320	// never claimed is harmless.
3321	// nextSeq returns the next per-id run sequence. It is only ever called while
3322	// the matching claim is held, so the read-modify-write is safe across
3323	// processes. The counter survives release and restarts, which makes it a
3324	// stable, collision-free basis for log file names.
3325	// claimIsStale reports whether a claim file is old enough to be abandoned.
3326	// An unreadable, unusable or old marker is treated as stale so it cannot wedge
3327	// the task permanently.
3328	// Freshness must come from the file's modification time, not from the stamp
3329	// written inside it: os.OpenFile(O_EXCL) creates an empty lock file before the
3330	// owner writes the stamp, and in that window a stamp-based check reads an empty
3331	// marker, treats it as malformed, and lets a second caller steal an in-flight
3332	// run. The stamp stays in the file for human inspection only; mtime is recorded
3333	// by the same create/write step and is already current when the file appears.
3334			// The marker vanished or cannot be inspected: do not wedge the task.
3335	// TestCronNextAcrossDSTTransitions guards the wall-clock hour jump in Next.
3336	// A DST fall-back makes one local hour occur twice; rebuilding the time with
3337	// time.Date at that hour resolves to the first occurrence, so an hour-advance
3338	// that ignores this can spin forever. Each case runs under a timeout so a
3339	// regression fails loudly instead of hanging the suite.
3340				// Regression: hour 1 is excluded, so Next must advance past BOTH
3341				// occurrences of 01:00 on the fall-back day. Rebuilding the time
3342				// at 01:00 pinned the loop to the first occurrence forever.
3343				// The repeated hour is allowed: the first occurrence is next.
3344				// 02:30 does not exist on the spring-forward day; the next valid
3345				// slot is the following morning.
3346				// Lord Howe shifts by 30 minutes, so a duration-based jump must
3347				// still land on a real wall-clock minute.
3348				// Jumping to the next hour must normalise the minute to zero.
3349	// TestCronNextAcrossMidnightDST guards the day/month jumps in Next. Zones that
3350	// shift DST at 00:00 have a nonexistent midnight, which time.Date normalises
3351	// backwards onto the previous day. Rebuilding a day boundary from that value
3352	// can leave Next spinning on the same excluded day forever (and, in the daemon,
3353	// wedge the store mutex because recordRun calls Next while holding it). Each
3354	// case runs under a timeout so a regression fails loudly instead of hanging.
3355				// The spring-forward day (2026-03-08) has no 00:00 in Havana;
3356				// a "day 1" task must resume from April rather than spin on March.
3357				// A weekday task starting on a Sunday during the transition must
3358				// reach Monday 09:30, not loop on the repeated 23:00 wall hour.
3359				// 01:00 on the transition day is the first valid instant after
3360				// the skipped midnight; the fallback step must land there rather
3361				// than skipping the day entirely.
3362				// Beirut also springs forward at midnight; a mid-month day must
3363				// land on the next month at its post-transition offset.
3364		// An hour is an elapsed hour, so the local clock jumps from 01:30 EST
3365		// to 03:30 EDT. Only day intervals are calendar-based.
3366		// An empty kind is inferred from the expression shape.
3367		// A multibyte (CJK) field before the bad value must not corrupt byte
3368		// offsets, and the same expression must always yield the same diagnostics.
3369		// "*" at 8, space at 9, "8" at 10.
3370		// A CJK-dominated 6-field expression reports the field-count issue first.
3371	// cronFieldNames are the canonical names of the five cron fields, in order.
3372	// cronWeekdayAliases maps common weekday spellings to the numeric value the
3373	// grammar expects. The grammar is numeric-only, so aliases are diagnosed rather
3374	// than parsed; the mapped value is used only to suggest a replacement.
3375	// CronIssue is one problem found by ValidateCron. Start and End are zero-based
3376	// byte offsets into the original expression, so they stay correct even when a
3377	// multibyte (for example CJK) character precedes the offending text.
3378	// String renders the issue as a single readable line with its position.
3379	// ValidateCron checks a five-field cron expression and returns every problem
3380	// it finds. Unlike ParseCron, which stops at the first error, ValidateCron
3381	// reports all issues together with the field name and byte position, so a CLI
3382	// or editor can point at the exact mistake.
3383	// It is a pure function: the result depends only on expr, and every diagnostic
3384	// is in English and independent of any CJK or other locale. Field counts,
3385	// ranges, steps, weekday aliases and illegal combinations (reversed ranges,
3386	// repeated step separators) are checked. A syntactically valid expression that
3387	// can never fire (for example day-of-month 31 restricted to February) is
3388	// reported as a warning. A valid expression returns an empty, non-nil slice.
3389		// Cross-field checks only make sense when all five fields are present and
3390		// structurally valid, so parsing cannot fail.
3391	// cronNeverFiresIssues warns when expr is syntactically valid but has no
3392	// matching time within five years. It reuses ParseCron/Next so the diagnostic
3393	// cannot drift from the scheduler's own definition of "fires".
3394	// cronFieldToken is one whitespace-separated field plus its byte offsets.
3395	// splitCronFields splits expr on Unicode whitespace while keeping byte
3396	// offsets. It matches strings.Fields semantics but remains CJK-independent:
3397	// offsets stay correct even when multibyte characters precede a bad field.
3398	// validateCronField reports every issue in a single field. base is the byte
3399	// offset of the field within the original expression.
3400				// Unrestricted: always valid.
3401	// validateCronValue validates one numeric value (or a weekday alias), returning
3402	// the parsed number and whether it is usable for range checks.
3403	// validateCronStep reports a non-positive or non-numeric step value.
3404	// Cron is a parsed five-field cron expression:
3405	//	minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week
3406	// Each field accepts `*`, a single value `n`, a range `a-b`, a step `*/n`,
3407	// a list `a,b,c`, and a stepped range `a-b/n`. Day of week 0 is Sunday and 7 is
3408	// accepted as Sunday too. Times are evaluated in the location of the time value
3409	// passed to Next.
3410	// Day-of-month and day-of-week follow the traditional Vixie rule: when both are
3411	// restricted (neither starts with `*`) a day matches if either field matches,
3412	// otherwise the restricted field must match.
3413	// fieldSet is a bitmap of allowed values; cron fields are always below 64.
3414	// Cron field bounds. Day of week is parsed as 0-7 so that 7 (=Sunday) is
3415	// accepted, then folded onto 0.
3416	// ParseCron parses a five-field cron expression.
3417		// Fold 7 onto 0 so both spellings of Sunday match.
3418	// parseCronField expands one field into a bitmap.
3419					// "5/15" means 5,20,35,50 in the traditional grammar.
3420	// Expression returns the normalised expression.
3421	// Matches reports whether t falls exactly on a cron slot (seconds are ignored).
3422	// matchesDay applies the day-of-month / day-of-week OR rule.
3423	// Next returns the first matching time strictly after t, truncated to the
3424	// minute. It returns the zero time when the expression cannot fire within five
3425	// years (for example "0 0 31 2 *").
3426				// Jump to the first minute of the next month.
3427				// Jump to midnight of the following day.
3428				// Jump to the start of the next wall-clock hour using duration
3429				// arithmetic, not time.Date at the same wall hour. During a DST
3430				// fall-back the local hour occurs twice and time.Date resolves to
3431				// the earlier occurrence, so rebuilding here would keep landing on
3432				// the same excluded hour forever. Adding the remaining minutes
3433				// advances in absolute time and terminates after at most two steps
3434				// (once for each occurrence of a repeated hour).
3435	// nextDayStart returns the first instant of the local day after t.
3436	// nextMonthStart returns the first instant of the local month after t.
3437	// Both rebuild a wall-clock boundary with time.Date, which is unsafe when the
3438	// zone shifts DST at midnight: a nonexistent 00:00 is normalised backwards onto
3439	// the previous day (for example 2026-03-08 00:00 in America/Havana becomes
3440	// 2026-03-07 23:00). Advancing to such a candidate can fail to move forward at
3441	// all, so Next would spin on the same excluded day forever. Falling back to an
3442	// absolute top-of-hour step keeps every iteration strictly increasing in real
3443	// time, which both terminates the loop and preserves the Vixie-style rule that
3444	// a midnight lost to DST has no firing slot that day.
3445	// forwardOrNextHour returns candidate when it is strictly after t, otherwise
3446	// an absolute step to the top of the following hour. The latter always moves
3447	// forward (by at least one minute) and is the same arithmetic the hour branch
3448	// of Next uses to walk past repeated or skipped wall-clock hours.
3449	// parseDays reports whether expr is a day-based interval ("1d", "2.5d",
3450	// "1 D") and returns the number of days. Day intervals advance the calendar,
3451	// not a fixed number of hours, so they keep the same wall-clock time across a
3452	// DST transition.

3454	// time and location across DST, then adds any fractional remainder as a plain
3455	// ParseInterval parses an interval expression: anything time.ParseDuration

3457	// Days are returned as 24h durations here for validation and logging; the
3458	// scheduler calls addCalendarDays for day expressions so they survive DST.
3459	// TestDaemonDoesNotRerunSlotHandledByAnotherProcess guards the shared-store
3460	// reload: a separate `automation run` process can handle a slot and advance
3461	// NextDue on disk while this daemon's in-memory copy still shows the old slot.
3462	// Without a refresh the daemon would claim and run that slot a second time.
3463		// Simulate a different process opening the same store file, running the
3464		// due task and advancing its next slot. Its writes go to disk only; the
3465		// daemon's cached Spec stays at the old 12:00 slot.
3466	// TestDaemonReloadPicksUpTaskAddedByAnotherProcess is the positive counterpart:
3467	// the refresh must also make tasks added to the file by another process visible
3468	// to the very next scan, rather than suppressing all work.
3469		// Another process adds an already-due task after the daemon was created.
3470	// TestDaemonRunDoesNotClobberConcurrentStoreEdits guards the write side of the
3471	// shared-store story. Reloading at scan start (see daemon_reload_test.go) keeps
3472	// a daemon from re-running a slot handled elsewhere, but the run outcome is
3473	// later saved from the in-memory snapshot taken at scan time. If another
3474	// process adds, edits or deletes a task while that run is in flight, saving the
3475	// whole list from the stale snapshot silently drops the additions and
3476	// resurrects the deletions. recordRun must refresh from disk before writing.
3477		// Block the executor so no further scan reload happens before the run
3478		// finishes: the only snapshot in play is the one scan() took above.
3479		// A separate process edits the shared task list while the run is in flight.
3480	// fakeClock is a manually advanced clock.
3481	// fakeExecutor records every call and can block or fail on demand.
3482		// The slot was 11:59, so the next one is 12:59 — advancing from the
3483		// completion time (12:00) would have produced 13:00 and caused drift.
3484				// With a 30s tick the daemon almost always sees a slot a few
3485				// seconds late. That is not "missed" and must still run.
3486		// A second scan with the same clock must find nothing left to do.
3487		// The slot is still in the past, so a second scan would fire again if the
3488		// daemon did not track in-flight runs.
3489		// A second slot must not inherit the first run's delay: 12:10 -> 12:20,
3490		// not 12:22. This is the long-term drift the probe reproduced.
3491		// Advancing from the stale 10:00 slot alone would yield 10:10 and fire
3492		// again immediately; the catch-up must resume from "now".
3493		// Glob returns sorted names; the later sequence is the second entry and
3494		// must be the one recorded as the most recent log.
3495	// Run statuses recorded in the store and in each run log.
3496	// DefaultTick is how often the daemon looks for due tasks.
3497	// Executor runs one task. The daemon supplies the task and honours its timeout;
3498	// the executor only has to turn a spec into a result (in the CLI that means
3499	// driving the agent loop, here it is an injected seam).
3500	// RunResult is the outcome of one run.
3501	// Success reports whether the run finished cleanly.
3502	// Options configures the daemon.
3503		// Store is the task list (required).
3504		// Executor runs due tasks (required).

3506		// defaults to <store dir>/runs.
3507		// Clock overrides time.Now; tests drive the schedule with it.
3508		// Tick is the polling interval; it defaults to DefaultTick.
3509	// daemon is the running scheduler.
3510		// startTime is when this daemon came up. A slot that was already due
3511		// before then had no chance to run in this process, so missed=skip may

3514	// Run polls the store until ctx is cancelled and returns nil on shutdown.
3515	// One scan happens immediately, so a daemon that starts after a slot was missed

3547		// Claim through the same store-level mechanism the daemon uses, so a

3580		// The time-of-day phrase. It always starts with either "at " or "every ".

3588	// consecutive runs into named ranges ("Monday-Friday", "January-March").
3589	// Package automation schedules agent prompts: a cron- or interval-based task
3590	// list, a durable JSON store, and a daemon that fires due tasks through an
3591	// injected executor.
3592	// A task is a small JSON document:
3593	//	{"id":"nightly","name":"nightly review",
3594	//	 "schedule":{"kind":"cron","expr":"0 3 * * *"},
3595	//	 "prompt":"review yesterday's commits","cwd":"/repo",
3596	//	 "enabled":true,"missed":"run_once","timeout":"30m"}
3597	// Everything here is sequential dependency-free Go: the daemon never spawns
3598	// the agent itself, it calls an Executor, so the CLI (or a test) wires the real
3599	// agent loop in afterwards.
3600	// Missed policies decide what happens when the daemon wakes up after a
3601	// scheduled slot has already passed.
3602		// MissedRunOnce runs the task once immediately, then resumes the schedule.
3603		// MissedSkip drops the missed slot and waits for the next one.
3604	// Duration is a JSON-friendly time.Duration. It marshals as a duration string
3605	// ("30m") and accepts either a duration string or a number of seconds when
3606	// decoding, so hand-written config files stay readable.
3607	// Std returns the value as a time.Duration.
3608	// String renders the duration the way time.Duration does.
3609	// Or returns the duration, or def when it is not positive.
3610	// MarshalJSON writes the duration as a string.
3611	// UnmarshalJSON accepts "45s", 90 (seconds), "" and null.
3612			*d = Duration(v)
3613		*d = Duration(time.Duration(seconds * float64(time.Second)))
3614	// Schedule says when a task fires.
3615		// Kind is "cron" or "interval". An empty kind is inferred from Expr.
3616		// Expr is a five-field cron expression or a Go duration ("30m", "1d").
3617	// Normalised schedule kinds.
3618	// kind resolves an empty or misspelled kind from the expression shape.
3619	// Validate reports whether the schedule can be parsed.
3620	// Next returns the first firing time strictly after t. A zero time means the
3621	// expression never fires (for example "0 0 31 2 *") or cannot be parsed.
3622			// "1d" means "same time tomorrow", so advance the calendar instead
3623			// of adding a fixed 24h, which drifts across DST transitions.
3624	// String renders the schedule for logs and for read-only task lists. The
3625	// canonical "kind expr" is always kept as a prefix; a valid cron expression
3626	// additionally gets a human-readable summary in parentheses so a CLI/TUI list
3627	// can show what the schedule actually means without a separate lookup.
3628	// Spec is one scheduled task plus its persisted run state.
3629		// Missed is "run_once" (default) or "skip".
3630		// NextDue is the slot the daemon is waiting for. It is seeded by the store
3631		// and advanced after every run, so a task never fires twice for one slot.
3632		// Warnings reports invisible characters found in the declarative content.
3633		// It is metadata only: it is never serialised and never blocks a task.
3634	// UnmarshalJSON applies the defaults a hand-written task file may omit:
3635	// enabled=true, missed="run_once", and an id derived from the name.
3636		*s = Spec{
3637	// MissedPolicy normalises the missed field; unknown values behave like
3638	// run_once so a typo never silently drops work.
3639	// Validate checks the fields the daemon relies on.
3640	// IsDue reports whether the task should run at now.
3641	// TimeoutOr returns the run timeout, falling back to def.
3642	// slug turns a task name into a filesystem-safe id.
3643	// storeFile is the on-disk shape of the task list.
3644	// Store is the durable task list: one JSON file in a directory, written
3645	// atomically (temp file + rename) and guarded by a mutex so concurrent
3646	// goroutines can read and write it safely.
3647	// OpenStore loads (or creates) the task list in dir.
3648	// Reload refreshes the in-memory task list from the on-disk JSON file. The
3649	// store is a shared file: a separate process (for example `automation run`)
3650	// can advance a slot or add a task while a daemon is running. Reloading lets
3651	// the daemon observe those changes instead of acting on a stale cached view,
3652	// which would fire an already-handled slot a second time.
3653	// reloadLocked parses the JSON file into s.specs. The caller must hold s.mu.
3654	// A missing file means an empty task list (a freshly created store).
3655	// Dir returns the store directory.
3656	// File returns the path of the JSON task list.
3657	// SetClock overrides the clock used to seed next-run times.
3658	// List returns a copy of every task, in insertion order.
3659	// Get returns one task.
3660	// Put validates and upserts a task, then persists the list. A task added
3661	// without a next-run time is seeded with the next slot from the store clock.
3662			// Keep the run history when the caller only supplied the definition.
3663	// Delete removes a task and persists the list.
3664	// SetEnabled flips a task on or off.
3665	// Due returns the enabled tasks whose next slot has passed.
3666	// RecordRun stores the outcome of one run and advances the next slot past at.
3667	// Manual runs have no scheduled slot, so the schedule resumes from at.
3668	// recordRun is RecordRun with the slot that triggered the run. When slot is
3669	// known, the next firing advances from that slot instead of from the (possibly
3670	// much later) completion time, so a slow task does not push the schedule
3671	// forward. at is still the time recorded as LastRun.
3672		// A run can outlive a scan, and the task list is a shared JSON file. While
3673		// the run was in flight another process may have added, edited or deleted
3674		// tasks. Saving the outcome from the in-memory snapshot taken at scan time
3675		// would clobber those edits (and resurrect a task deleted mid-run), because
3676		// saveLocked rewrites the whole list. Refresh from disk while holding the
3677		// lock so this is a read-modify-write of the current file. A task deleted
3678		// meanwhile simply disappears from the list, which callers already treat as
3679		// "nothing to record".
3680			// The slot we just advanced to is already in the past (the run
3681			// started late or lasted longer than the interval). Resume from the
3682			// run time so a run_once task does not fire again on the next tick.
3683	// Save rewrites the task list.
3684	// writeAtomic writes data to a temporary file in the destination directory and
3685	// renames it into place, so a reader never sees a half-written file.
3686		// An unquoted [...] is normalised by the frontmatter parser into a bare
3687		// comma-joined scalar (the brackets are dropped), yet the candidates are
3688		// still recovered from the '|' separators.
3689		// Each whitespace-delimited token is inspected on its own; a single value
3690		// enclosed in a wrapper must not leak in just because another token is a
3691		// real enumeration.
3692	// Skill argument-hint completion metadata.
3693	// SkillArgumentHints turns the declarative `argument-hint` frontmatter of a set
3694	// of skills into enumerated candidate values keyed by skill name. A frontend
3695	// (for example a slash command that invokes a skill) can use the map to offer
3696	// live argument suggestions without parsing frontmatter or duplicating the
3697	// package's hint grammar. The helper is pure: it reads only its argument, never
3698	// touches the filesystem and never mutates the skills it is given.
3699	// The hint grammar mirrors slash-command argument hints. A whitespace-delimited
3700	// token containing '|' is an enumerated choice, e.g. `dev|stage|prod` or
3701	// `[dev|stage|prod]`; one optional <...> or [...] wrapper is stripped first.
3702	// Plain placeholders such as `<file>` describe an open-ended value and yield
3703	// no candidates. Full-width ASCII (including the full-width '|' and brackets)
3704	// is folded to half-width so hints authored through a Chinese IME are read the
3705	// same way as their ASCII spelling.
3706	// SkillArgumentHints returns the enumerated argument candidates of every skill
3707	// that declares at least one, keyed by skill name.
3708	//   - Only tokens that, after stripping one optional <...> or [...] wrapper,
3709	//     contain '|' contribute; a group needs two or more distinct values to
3710	//     count as an enumeration.
3711	//   - Empty alternatives and duplicates are dropped; order follows the hint.
3712	//   - Skills with no hint, or with only open placeholders, get no entry, so a
3713	//     lookup for them returns a nil slice.
3714	//   - Duplicate names: the last occurrence wins (ordinary map semantics).
3715	//   - A nil or empty input yields an empty, non-nil map.
3716	// argumentHintCandidates extracts the enumerated values from one argument-hint.
3717	// foldHintWidth folds full-width ASCII and the ideographic space into their
3718	// half-width forms, mirroring slash-command completion so a hint typed through
3719	// a Chinese IME is recognised. CJK characters themselves are left untouched.
3720				// A partially written document would be skipped by Parse.
3721	// ErrInvalidSkillName is returned when a name is not lowercase kebab-case or
3722	// tries to escape its skills root.
3723	// ErrSkillExists is returned when a skill already exists at the target path
3724	// and the caller did not opt into overwriting it.
3725	// maxNameLength bounds authored names so a model cannot create absurd paths.
3726	// AuthorOptions configures AuthorWithOptions.
3727		// Overwrite permits replacing an existing SKILL.md at the target path.
3728	// AuthorResult is the outcome of a successful authoring operation.
3729		// Path is the SKILL.md file that was written.
3730		// Name is the validated skill name.
3731	// authorMu serialises authors within one process so the no-overwrite check is
3732	// reliable when several callers race to create the same skill. Discovery in
3733	// this package is safe without the lock because publishing uses temp+rename.
3734	// Author writes a well-formed SKILL.md for name under root and returns its
3735	// path. With an empty root the user's skill directory is used. Existing skills
3736	// are never replaced; use AuthorWithOptions with Overwrite to allow that.
3737	// The signature deliberately uses only stdlib types so an agent package can
3738	// declare an identical local interface without importing this one.
3739	// AuthorWithOptions is Author with explicit options.
3740	// validateName enforces lowercase kebab-case with no path traversal. The
3741	// character rules alone reject dots, separators and uppercase, but the
3742	// explicit ".." check gives callers a clearer diagnostic.
3743	// authorTarget resolves the absolute location of a new skill. A non-empty root
3744	// is a workspace (project skills); an empty root uses the user's home skills.
3745	// renderSkill builds the document. name is validated before it is embedded,
3746	// so it needs no quoting; description is written as a single-quoted YAML
3747	// scalar with doubled quotes and newlines folded to spaces, which round-trips
3748	// through ParseFields exactly and cannot break out of the frontmatter block.
3749	// publishSkill writes to a temp file in the same directory and renames it
3750	// into place. A concurrent Discover therefore sees either the old file or the
3751	// complete new one, never a half-written document.
3752	// Fields holds the values parsed from a YAML-ish frontmatter block.
3753	// Only the subset a skill or command file needs is supported: `key: scalar`,
3754	// quoted scalars, `key: [a, b]` inline lists, indented `- item` lists and the
3755	// `>` / `|` block scalars. There is deliberately no YAML dependency: the input
3756	// is hand-written markdown, and a full parser would accept documents we do not
3757	// want to read anyway.
3758	// ParseFields parses the text between the two `---` fences.
3759	// Keys are lower-cased. A repeated key keeps its last value. Lines that are
3760	// not `key: value` and are not part of an indented block are ignored, so a
3761	// stray prose line cannot fail a whole document.
3762				// An indented line with no owning key: part of a block already
3763				// consumed, or junk. Either way there is nothing to attach it to.
3764	// Get returns the scalar value for key, or "" when it is absent.
3765	// List returns the values for key. A scalar `a, b` is split on commas, so both
3766	// `tools: [a, b]` and `tools:\n  - a\n  - b` behave the same way.
3767	// Keys returns every key seen, sorted.
3768		// Trailing blank lines belong to the next field, not this block.
3769	// blockText renders a `>`, `|` or plain indented block. Trailing blank lines
3770	// are always dropped; chomping indicators (`>-`, `|-`) therefore mean the same
3771	// thing as their plain forms.
3772			// Decode escapes left-to-right. A chained ReplaceAll would rewrite
3773			// `\n` before `\\`, turning the literal `\\n` (backslash + "n") into a
3774			// newline and corrupting values such as a Windows path in quotes.
3775					// Unknown escape: keep both bytes verbatim.
3776	// SplitFrontmatter splits a leading `---` block from the body.
3777	// The opening fence must be the first line and the closing fence a line of its
3778	// own. A file without a block returns ok=false and the caller decides whether
3779	// that is an error (skills) or acceptable (commands).
3780	// writeResource creates a file at rel inside dir (creating parent directories)
3781	// and returns its byte size.
3782		// Sorting must be deterministic in path order.
3783	// ResourceInfo describes one auxiliary file shipped alongside a skill's
3784	// SKILL.md (for example a script under scripts/ or a document under
3785	// references/). Path is a slash-separated path relative to the skill
3786	// directory; Size is the file size in bytes. Callers use this manifest to tell
3787	// a model which files it can read or run after the skill has been loaded.
3788	// ListSkillResources lists the auxiliary files under skillDir. It is a
3789	// read-only scan with no dependencies and no global state: the result depends
3790	// only on the directory contents and maxBytes.
3791	//   - the skill document itself (SKILL.md or SKILL.markdown, case-insensitive,
3792	//     at any depth) is not a resource and is omitted;
3793	//   - hidden files and directories (names starting with '.') are skipped, and
3794	//     hidden directories are never descended into;
3795	//   - symlinks that resolve outside skillDir are skipped, as are symlinks to
3796	//     anything other than a regular file. Symlinks that stay inside are listed
3797	//     under the link's own relative path with the size of their target;
3798	//   - only regular files are listed;
3799	//   - results are sorted by Path;
3800	//   - maxBytes is the total byte budget. Files are accumulated in path order
3801	//     and any file that would overflow the remaining budget is skipped, which
3802	//     also excludes any single file larger than maxBytes. A non-positive
3803	//     maxBytes yields no resources.
3804	// Unreadable entries are skipped rather than failing the whole scan.
3805	// isSkillDocument reports whether name is a skill document rather than a
3806	// resource. Matching is case-insensitive so a differently-cased duplicate on
3807	// Windows cannot leak into the manifest.
3808	// resourceSize returns the size of the regular file p represents. Symlinks are
3809	// resolved; ones escaping root or pointing at a non-regular file are rejected,
3810	// while ones that stay inside are reported with their target's size.
3811	// pathWithin reports whether target is root itself or lives beneath it. It is
3812	// the containment check that rejects symlinks escaping the skill directory.
3813		// The span kept for "same" is the first occurrence, not the last.
3814	// MaxShellCommands caps how many distinct shell commands a single skill body
3815	// may expose. Extra unique commands past this limit are ignored, which bounds
3816	// both the approval queue and the work a caller can be tricked into doing.
3817	// ShellKillSwitchEnv is the environment variable a caller checks to disable
3818	// skill shell injection outright. This package never reads the environment;
3819	// the gate is the pure ShellInjectAllowed function below.
3820	// Span is a half-open byte range [Start, End) into the skill body that was
3821	// handed to ParseShellCommands. It covers the whole executable token, i.e. the
3822	// opening backtick, the leading '!', the command, and the closing backtick.
3823	// Byte offsets (not rune offsets) match Go's string slicing and regexp indices.
3824	// ParseShellCommands scans a skill body once and returns the shell commands
3825	// written in executable position plus the span each one occupied.
3826	// The only executable form is an inline code span whose first character is
3827	// '!': `!git status` yields "git status". Inert regions are skipped entirely:
3828	//   - fenced code blocks (``` ... ```), including their opening and closing
3829	//   - ordinary inline code (`echo hi`) that does not start with '!';
3830	//   - backslashed backticks (\`), which are escaped and never delimit a span.
3831	// Parsing is deliberately conservative: a stray fence or an unterminated
3832	// inline span only ever suppresses a command, never invents one. Scanning is
3833	// rune-aware (so CJK content cannot corrupt offsets) and this function never
3834	// executes anything. Duplicate commands are dropped, keeping the first
3835	// occurrence, and at most MaxShellCommands unique commands are returned.
3836				// An escaped rune is literal: skip the backslash and its target so
3837				// an escaped backtick cannot open an inline span.
3838					// Fenced block: inert until the matching closing fence (or EOF).
3839					// No closing backtick: treat the opening one as literal text.
3840	// findClosingBacktick returns the byte index of the first unescaped backtick
3841	// at or after from. Escaped backticks (\`) are not delimiters.
3842	// ShellInjectAllowed is the gate for acting on parsed commands. Commands may
3843	// only be surfaced for execution when the skill is trusted and the kill-switch
3844	// has not been set. It is pure: reading KILO_DISABLE_SKILL_SHELL and deciding
3845	// trust are the caller's responsibilities.
3846	// YAML double-quoted scalars process escapes left-to-right: "\\\\" is a single
3847	// literal backslash, so a following "n" or "t" is ordinary text. A chain of
3848	// ReplaceAll calls processes "\n" before "\\\\" and corrupts an escaped
3849	// Windows path such as "C:\\temp\\name" into tabs and newlines.
3850				// Header plus exactly one line for the skill.
3851	// Package skills discovers SKILL.md procedures on disk and loads them on demand.
3852	// A skill is a directory containing `SKILL.md` (a bare `<name>.md` is accepted
3853	// too). The file starts with a `---` frontmatter block carrying at least `name`
3854	// and `description`; the rest is the procedure itself.
3855	// Only the compact index (one line per skill) belongs in the system prompt; the
3856	// body is fetched through the `use_skill` tool. Twenty installed skills then
3857	// cost twenty lines instead of twenty documents.
3858	// DefaultProjectSubdir is where a repository keeps its own skills.
3859	// DefaultHomeSubdir is where the user keeps skills shared by every project.
3860	// ErrNotFound is returned by Load when no skill carries the requested name.
3861	// Skill is one procedure on disk.
3862		// Name is what the model passes to the tool.
3863		// Description is the one-line trigger summary shown in the index.
3864		// WhenToUse is the longer "load me when…" hint, also shown in the index.
3865		// ArgumentHint is the usage hint from the frontmatter `argument-hint` field.
3866		// It carries optional enumerated candidates used by SkillArgumentHints.
3867		// Body is the procedure without frontmatter.
3868		// Path is the SKILL.md file the procedure came from.
3869		// Tools names script tools the skill carries (frontmatter `tools`).
3870		// AllowedTools is the tool allow-list advertised by the skill.
3871		// Warnings reports invisible characters found in the declarative content.
3872		// It is metadata only: parsing never changes and never blocks on it.
3873		// Triggers holds optional automatic-activation rules. It is metadata only:
3874		// nothing here changes how the skill loads by name through use_skill.
3875	// Entry is the compact form of a skill used by the index and by Match.
3876		// Score is only set by Match; it is never serialised.
3877	// Entry returns the compact index form of a skill.
3878	// Parse reads one skill document. path is used for diagnostics and as the
3879	// fallback name (the directory name for `.../foo/SKILL.md`).
3880	// DefaultRoots returns the skill directories searched for one session, in
3881	// priority order: the project first, then the user's home directory.
3882	// Registry is the set of skills available to one session.
3883	// New returns an empty registry.
3884	// Discover loads every skill under roots.
3885	// Roots are searched in order and a later root never replaces an earlier one,
3886	// so a project skill shadows the user's skill with the same name. A directory
3887	// or file that fails to parse is skipped: one broken skill must not hide the
3888	// Add registers a skill, replacing any existing skill with the same name.
3889	// All returns every skill, sorted by name.
3890	// Len returns the number of installed skills.
3891	// Get finds one skill by exact name.
3892	// Index returns the compact entries for the system prompt.
3893	// IndexText renders the index as the block appended to the system prompt.
3894	// It is empty when no skill is installed.
3895			// Declarative metadata is untrusted and may come from a block scalar or
3896			// a quoted \n escape. Fold it so each skill still occupies exactly one
3897			// index line and cannot inject entries into the system prompt.
3898	// oneLine collapses embedded whitespace so a single skill cannot emit more
3899	// than one line in a plain-text rendering. Parse intentionally preserves
3900	// newlines in block scalars, so the folding belongs at the rendering boundary.
3901	// Load returns the full skill, body included.
3902	// Match ranks skills by keyword overlap with prompt.
3903	// The score counts distinct prompt words that also appear in the name (weight
3904	// 4), the when_to_use hint (weight 3) or the description (weight 2). Only
3905	// skills with a positive score are returned, best first, then by name.
3906	// Suggest lists skill names loosely related to name, for a miss hint.
3907	// stopwords keeps filler words from creating matches on their own.
3908	// keywords lower-cases s and splits it into distinct word tokens, dropping
3909	// filler words and single characters.
3910	// ToolSpec describes a tool the caller can register with its own registry.
3911	// It mirrors the wire shape every provider expects (`type: function`,
3912	// `function: {name, description, parameters}`) but is deliberately a local type
3913	// so this package does not depend on the agent or llm packages.
3914	// ToolSchema is the function half of a ToolSpec.
3915	// UseSkillSpec returns the tool the caller should advertise so the model can
3916	// load a skill body by name.
3917	// UseSkillSpec is the same spec bound to a registry, for callers that build it
3918	// per session.
3919	// HandleUseSkill executes the use_skill tool with raw JSON arguments.
3920	// An empty or missing `name` lists the installed skills; a known name returns
3921	// the full body with its source path; an unknown name returns an error whose
3922	// message suggests close matches.
3923		// Insertion order is intentionally not sorted: the aggregate must keep it.
3924	// Triggers holds optional automatic-activation rules for a skill.
3925	// Each list is an independent filter and an empty list means "no restriction":
3926	// a skill with zero triggers is eligible everywhere. Callers combine the
3927	// filters that fit their event (a file path, a prompt, a model family) instead
3928	// of relying on the model to call use_skill by name. Matching here is pure: no
3929	// filesystem access and no dependencies beyond the standard library.
3930		// Paths are gitignore-style globs matched against the path of a file the
3931		// agent is about to read or edit.
3932		// Keywords are case-insensitive substrings matched against prompt text.
3933		// Models restricts the skill to these model families (e.g. "claude",
3934		// "gemini"). An empty list, "all" or "*" means every family.
3935	// MatchPath reports whether p matches any of the gitignore-style globs in
3936	// Syntax: `**` spans zero or more path segments, `*` stays inside one segment,
3937	// and a pattern without a slash is tested against the basename at any depth.
3938	// Patterns and p are normalised the same way: backslashes become slashes, a
3939	// leading `./` or `/` is dropped and comparison is case-insensitive. One rule
3940	// therefore works for both Windows and POSIX paths.
3941	// matchSegments walks pattern and segments together. A `**` pattern segment
3942	// consumes zero or more path segments; every other segment is handed to
3943	// path.Match, which owns the `*` / `?` / `[...]` wildcard syntax.
3944	// normalizeGlobPath makes a pattern or a concrete path comparable: one
3945	// separator, no leading marker and one case. Lower-casing keeps Windows-style
3946	// case-insensitive paths working without a build tag or a runtime check.
3947	// SkillsForPath returns the skills eligible for a file path: those with no
3948	// path triggers plus those whose path triggers match p. Registry order is kept.
3949	// MatchKeywords reports whether text contains any of the case-insensitive
3950	// keywords. Substring matching keeps CJK keywords working, where word-based
3951	// tokenisation would drop them.
3952	// SkillsForKeywords returns the skills eligible for prompt text: those with no
3953	// keyword triggers plus those with a keyword hit. Registry order is kept.
3954	// SkillsForModel returns the skills available to a model family. family is
3955	// compared case-insensitively; skills with no model restriction (or with
3956	// "all" / "*") are available to every family. Registry order is kept.
3957	// ResolveTriggeredSkills is the single entry point for automatic skill
3958	// activation. It describes one agent event with a file path, prompt text and
3959	// model family and returns every skill eligible for that event.
3960	// The three trigger dimensions are independent constraints: a skill is
3961	// eligible only when each list it declares is either empty (no restriction)
3962	// or satisfied by the matching argument. In other words the path, keyword and
3963	// model filters are intersected, never unioned — a model restriction must not
3964	// be bypassed just because a keyword happened to match. An empty argument
3965	// therefore excludes skills restricted to that dimension, so with no context
3966	// only unrestricted skills (plus model-wildcard skills) remain.
3967	// Registry order is preserved (each filter only removes entries) and no name
3968	// is returned twice. A nil registry yields nil.
3969	// resolveTriggeredSkills is the slice-based core of ResolveTriggeredSkills.
3970	// Keeping it separate lets the de-duplication guarantee be exercised directly
3971	// with a hand-built slice (a Registry already enforces unique names).
3972	// Auth resolves the credential for this template. The caller owns the header
3973	// name and value scheme (Authorization/Bearer for OpenAI-compatible endpoints,
3974	// x-api-key/empty for Anthropic) and the environment lookup, so config and app
3975	// can adopt the chain without the provider table depending on either.
3976	// NoAuth wins over every credential source. Otherwise the first non-empty
3977	// value among APIKeyEnv and APIKeyEnvs wins; with no value the result is the
3978	// llm.NoAuth sentinel and the caller writes no header.
3979	// Package provider model-catalog refresh strategy.
3980	// This file ports the policy half of omp's model-manager.ts without any of its
3981	// I/O: no HTTP discovery, no cache database, no clock/side effects. A caller
3982	// owns the actual fetch and persistence; it calls ShouldRefresh to decide
3983	// whether to fetch, Merge to combine a fetched roster with the static table,
3984	// and FingerprintStatic to invalidate caches written against an older static
3985	// catalog. Keeping the policy pure makes it deterministic and safe to call from
3986	// any goroutine.
3987	// Default catalog freshness knobs, matching omp's DEFAULT_CACHE_TTL_MS (2h)
3988	// and NON_AUTHORITATIVE_RETRY_MS (5min). Callers may pass their own values.
3989	// RefreshPolicy controls when dynamic model discovery is allowed to run.
3990	// It mirrors omp's ModelRefreshStrategy.
3991	// Supported refresh strategies.
3992		// RefreshOnline always refreshes from the dynamic source, even when a
3993		// fresh authoritative snapshot exists.
3994		// RefreshOffline never refreshes; only static data and an existing cache
3995		// are used.
3996		// RefreshOnlineIfUncached refreshes when there is no snapshot, when the
3997		// snapshot exceeded its TTL, or when a non-authoritative snapshot aged past
3998		// the short retry window. This is the default strategy.
3999	// Model is the minimal catalog row the policy layer needs: a stable identity
4000	// for merging/de-duplicating and a display name so a discovered row can carry
4001	// a new name over a static one. The full wire model lives in internal/llm;
4002	// callers map between the two at the integration boundary.
4003	// CatalogSnapshot is a point-in-time view of a provider's model catalog.
4004	// Authoritative reports that the snapshot came from a complete discovery
4005	// result (not a fallback after a failed fetch); Stale marks a snapshot that
4006	// must not enjoy the full TTL. FetchedAt is zero when no snapshot has ever
4007	// been stored.
4008	// ParseRefreshPolicy normalizes a config/CLI string into a RefreshPolicy.
4009	// The boolean reports whether the value was recognized.
4010	// ShouldRefresh decides whether dynamic discovery should run now.
4011	// Policy semantics:
4012	//   - offline: never.
4013	//   - online: always.
4014	//   - online-if-uncached (and any unrecognized policy, treated as the
4015	//     conservative default): refresh when there is no snapshot, when its age
4016	//     exceeds ttl, or when it is non-authoritative and its age reached
4017	// A snapshot counts as authoritative only when Authoritative is true and Stale
4018	// is false, so a stale snapshot is refreshed on the short retry window rather
4019	// than waiting out the full TTL. A zero FetchedAt means "uncached". Negative
4020	// ages (a clock moved backwards) are clamped to zero. Passing a non-positive
4021	// staleRetryWindow makes non-authoritative snapshots refresh immediately.
4022	// Merge overlays discovered models on the static catalog by ID. Static rows
4023	// keep their order; a discovered row with a known ID replaces that row in
4024	// place, and discovered-only IDs are appended. Empty IDs are ignored, and the
4025	// last duplicate of an ID wins (matching the map-based merge in omp). The
4026	// returned slice is fresh and never aliases either input.
4027	// FingerprintStatic returns a stable content identity for a static catalog.
4028	// A cached snapshot written against one fingerprint must be discarded when
4029	// the current fingerprint differs (a static-table upgrade or policy change).
4030	// Length-prefixing each field avoids ambiguity between model ids and names.
4031	// probeFrom builds a deterministic, injected probe: every URL in failed is
4032	// reported unhealthy, everything else is healthy. It performs no network I/O
4033	// and is safe for concurrent reads, so the same probe can be reused by the
4034	// purity test below.
4035		// The caller's input must never be modified by a probe run.
4036	// Package provider endpoint health probing.
4037	// This file ports the endpoint-filtering half of opencode's vLLM provider
4038	// health check without any of its I/O: no HTTP client, no timers, no side
4039	// effects. A caller owns the actual probe (an HTTP GET to <baseURL>/health, a
4040	// TCP dial, or anything else) and injects it as a function. ProbeHealthy
4041	// applies that policy to a candidate list of base URLs and returns the subset
4042	// that answered healthy. Keeping the policy pure makes it deterministic,
4043	// order-preserving, and safe to call from any goroutine.
4044	// ProbeHealthy filters baseURLs down to the endpoints that pass an injected
4045	// For each unique URL, in input order, probe(url) is called exactly once: a
4046	// nil error marks the endpoint healthy and it is kept, any non-nil error drops
4047	// it. Duplicate URLs are probed once and appear at most once in the result, at
4048	// the position of their first occurrence. The returned slice is freshly
4049	// allocated and never aliases baseURLs; a nil or empty input yields an empty
4050	// (non-nil) slice.
4051	// probe is always invoked sequentially on the calling goroutine, so it need
4052	// not be concurrency-safe; it only has to be safe to call repeatedly. A nil
4053	// probe is a no-op filter: every unique URL is treated as healthy, which is
4054	// useful to callers that only want de-duplication.
4055		// One representative from every ecosystem plus the guidance files that a
4056		// repo map should pin regardless of symbol budget.
4057		// README/license/Dockerfile/Makefile variants share a prefix plus a name
4058		// separator, so every spelling and extension is recognized.
4059		// CJK directory segments are irrelevant; the base name decides.
4060	// IsImportantProjectFile reports whether path names a high-value project file
4061	// that a repository map should keep even when a symbol/token budget would
4062	// otherwise crowd it out (the aider "important files first" guarantee).
4063	// It is a pure classification helper: no I/O, no environment, no mutation of
4064	// its input. Matching is case-insensitive and looks only at the file's base
4065	// name, so directory prefixes (including CJK path segments and either slash
4066	// style) do not affect the result. It is meant to be called by the repo-map
4067	// tool when deciding which entries to pin into the map; keeping the policy here
4068	// lets that decision stay consistent across frontends without importing config.
4069	// Two rules are combined:
4070	//   - an exact set of well-known manifest / metadata file names;
4071	//   - a set of "families" matched on a base-name prefix followed by a name
4072	//     separator (., -, _), covering README/license/Dockerfile/Makefile variants.
4073	// CI workflow files under a .github/workflows directory are always important
4074	// regardless of their individual file name.
4075		// Normalize both slash styles so Windows and POSIX paths classify the same.
4076		// CI workflows are important by location, not by file name.
4077		// Extract the base name, ignoring trailing slashes and "."/".." segments.
4078	// matchesFileFamily reports whether base is prefix itself or starts with
4079	// prefix followed by a name separator. The separator requirement avoids false
4080	// positives such as "licenseplate.go" for the "license" family while keeping
4081	// README.md, README-zh.md, Dockerfile.dev and LICENSE-MIT.
4082	// importantProjectFiles is the exact, case-folded set of manifest, metadata and
